Statistics Annual Reports (2018-2019). https://pub.azdhs.gov/health-stats/report/ahs/ 
6 Sum rounded to nearest tens unit (due to addend of county count less than 10). 
7 https://www.cdc.gov/drugoverdose/deaths/2019.html

<!-- page 212 -->
10 
 
 
Definitions 
Opioids 
Opioids are a class of drugs that include the illegal drug heroin, synthetic opioids such as 
fentanyl, and pain relievers available legally by prescription, such as oxycodone (OxyContin®), 
hydrocodone (Vicodin®), codeine, morphine, and many others. 
7F
8 
 
Opioid Deaths 
Opioid death events in Arizona are based upon final determination of cause of death as 
reported on the official certificate of death. The underlying cause code used in opioid 
overdose deaths may not always be specific to opioids, in which case the contributing 
cause(s) are also referenced. For example, drug overdose deaths caused by acute poisonings 
as the underlying cause of death that involve any opioid as a contributing cause of death, 
regardless of intent (e.g., unintentional, suicide, assault, or undetermined) or type of drug are 
considered opioid deaths. The list of ICD-10 codes used to identify opioid deaths can be found 
here
. 
 
Non-Fatal Opioid Overdose 
Suspected opioid overdoses with confirmed or probable case classifications. To meet the 
case classification, various criteria are taken into consideration in addition to the toxicology 
report. 
 
Suspected Drug Overdose Categories for Syndromic Surveillance 
Standardized syndrome definitions, developed by the Centers for Disease Control and 
Prevention (CDC), are used for suspected all drug, all opioid, heroin, and stimulant overdoses. 
More information can be found here
. 
 
Syndromic Surveillance 
Syndromic surveillance provides public health officials with a timely system for detecting, 
understanding, and monitoring health events. By tracking symptoms and diagnosis 
information of patients (syndromes) in participating emergency and inpatient departments, 
public health can detect unusual levels of illness to determine whether a response is 
warranted. Visits are classified by the type of facility the person visited (i.e., ED or inpatient 
hospitalization). 
                                                             
8 https://www.cdc.gov/opioids/data/index.html

<!-- page 213 -->
11 
 
Data Sources 
Hospital Discharge Data 
ADHS collects hospital discharge records for ED and hospitalizations from all Arizona licensed 
hospitals. This collection is required by Arizona Revised Statute (A.R.S.) § 36-125-05, and 
Arizona Administrative Code Title 9, Chapter 11, Articles 4 and 5. Hospital discharge data 
included both fatal and non-fatal encounters. Data were submitted by 144 facilities in Arizona 
(as of December, 2020). The list of ICD-10 codes used to identify opioid deaths can be found 
here. 
 
MEDSIS 
Medical Electronic Disease Surveillance Intelligence System (MEDSIS) is used by healthcare 
professionals, healthcare institutions, correctional facilities, and medical examiners to report 
suspected opioid overdose events. Only non-fatal opioid overdose related events with 
confirmed or probable case classification are included. 
 
Death Certificates 
Information on opioid deaths (see definition above) is compiled from the original documents 
filed with the Arizona Department of Health Services Vital Statistics and from transcripts of 
original death certificates filed in other states but affecting Arizona residents. Death 
certificate data used for this report was provisional at the time of report development (i.e., 
death data was not finalized by ADHS when the data was extracted for analysis). 
 
Emergency and Inpatient Visits Data 
The BioSense Platform
- In partnership with local and state jurisdictions, the CDC hosts a 
secure, cloud-based, integrated electronic health information system to store syndromic 
surveillance data called the BioSense Platform. Participating health departments can access 
their jurisdiction’s facility information through the platform. Syndromic surveillance visits 
include fatal or non-fatal encounters. Data used meet the criteria for “suspected drug 
overdose categories for syndromic surveillance” define above. 
 
AZ-PIERS 
The Arizona Pre-Hospital Information & EMS Registry System (AZ-PIERS) is a web-based 
prehospital patient care data repository supported by the ADHS Bureau of EMS & Trauma 
System (B EMSTS ). Emergency Medical Services and Law Enforcement use AZ-PIERS to report 
any out-of-hospital suspected opioid overdoses, suspected opioid overdose deaths, and out-
of-hospital use of naloxone to treat opioid overdoses.

<!-- page 214 -->
12 
 
Prescription Drug Monitoring Program (PDMP) 
Arizona State Board of Pharmacy’s Controlled Substances Prescription Monitoring Program 
(CSPMP) requires pharmacies and medical practitioners who dispense Schedule II, III, and IV 
controlled substances to a patient, to report prescription information to the PDMP on a daily 
basis. This report only includes data for prescriptions dispensed among Arizona residents. As 
pertaining to prescriptions dispensed, we also excluded Schedule I and V controlled 
substances, buprenorphine drugs that were not Butrans® or Belbuca®, and values greater 
than 2,000 for quantity dispensed (presumed to be erroneous).  
 
Analytic Methods 
Prevalence Estimates 
The prevalence for the number of fatal and non-fatal opioid related events were calculated as 
the proportion of persons who had an opioid overdose at a specified point in time.  
 
Denominators 
The denominators for rates of fatal and non-fatal opioid related events and hospital 
encounters were calculated based on 2017-2020 population data from ADHS
. For 2021 
calculations, 2020 population data was used (because 2021 population data was not yet 
available at the time of report development). Denominators for ED visits (syndromic 
surveillance), Emergency Medical Services (EMS), Naloxone administration, and prescriptions 
filled were data source specific, and based on the number of events. 
 
Trend Analyses 
Prevalence estimates were examined over multiple years (and across groups) to compare 
current and past trends to provide insight into population differences over time.

<!-- page 215 -->
13 
 
Findings 
The findings described in this section are derived from several sources, including hospital 
discharges, death certificates, various surveillance systems, and stakeholder inputs. It is 
important to note that ADHS suppresses numbers less than six to protect confidentiality of 
rare cases and to eliminate bias or room for error in reporting numbers or rates.  
 
Hospital Discharges and Costs (Data: Hospital Discharge Data) 
Trends 2016-2021 
The number and associated costs of opioid-related hospitalizations have been fairly stable 
from 2020-2021, subsequent to a significant increase in the number and associated costs of 
opioid-related hospitalizations from 2016-2019. In 2020-2021, there were 51,943 and 52,507 
opioid-related hospitalizations, respectively, with a 0-1% annual net change in associated 
costs.

Disposition Comparison 
The majority of opioid-related hospitalization and ED visits had a disposition of routine 
discharge (68,134, (65%)) or discharged for further care (35,178 (34%)). Visits with a 
disposition resulting in death (1,138 (1%)) were associated with higher costs per encounter 
and a longer length of stay.

Drugs Involved in Non-Fatal Opioid Overdose Events 
Fentanyl was the most common drug reported that was involved in non-fatal opioid overdose 
events during 2020-2021. Polysubstance (typically defined as the consumption of more than 
one drug at once) was reported in about a third of non-fatal opioid overdose events during 
2020-2021.

Opioid Overdose Deaths Among Arizona Residents (Data: Death 
Certificates) 
Trends 2017-2021 
The total number of opioid overdose deaths among Arizona residents increased from 2017-
2021 (+120%). The number of prescription/synthetic drugs and polysubstance drug deaths 
increased from 2017-2021 (+75%). From 2020-2021 the increase in total opioid deaths was 
minimal (+6%). The number of heroin drug deaths decreased from 2017-2021 (-49%).

Emergency Department (ED) and Inpatient Visits Related to Suspected 
Opioid Overdoses (Data: Syndromic Surveillance) 
Syndromic surveillance provides public health officials with a timely system for detecting, 
understanding, and monitoring health events. By tracking symptoms and diagnosis 
information of patients (syndromes) in participating emergency and inpatient departments, 
public health can detect unusual levels of illness to determine whether a response is 
warranted. The number of facilities included in the calculation of monthly estimates will vary 
over time. In Arizona over 100 facilities are participating (~90% of EDs) reporting 
approximately 10,000 hospital visits every day. Diagnoses and chief complaints are used to 
query the occurrence of drug classes.  
Trends 2020-2021 
The number of opioid overdose ED and inpatient visits reported by syndromic surveillance 
was fairly stable from 2020-2021. The number of opioid overdose ED and inpatient visits 
reported by syndromic surveillance was highest in the summer months (June-August) during 
2020, but highest in the spring months (March-May) during 2021.

2020-Decmber 2021

with hospital discharge data (HDD).

EMS/Law Enforcement Administered Naloxone 
Out-of-hospital suspected opioid overdoses, suspected opioid overdose deaths, and out-of-
hospital use of naloxone to treat opioid overdoses is reported through the web-based 
prehospital patient care data repository (AZ-PIERS). ADHS has naloxone available at no cost 
for law enforcement agencies, county health departments, hospital and medical center EDs, 
and community-based organizations (i.e., substance use coalitions, harm reduction 
organizations, family and homeless shelters. 
 
The number of EMS/law enforcement responses for suspected opioid overdoses with 
naloxone administered varied monthly from 2020-2021 (total doses = 15,012), coinciding with 
the months when the number of suspected opioid overdoses are highest and lowest. The 
percent of EMS/law enforcement responses for suspected opioid overdoses with naloxone 
administered was fairly consistently above 80% (range 77-90%) during 2020-2021. There are 
situations where naloxone is appropriately not administered, an thus the goal for naloxone 
administration is not 100%.

<!-- page 257 -->
55 
 
Limitations 
Several limitations should be kept in mind when reviewing data included in this report.  
 
Rate Calculations 
Populations rates for fatal and non-fatal opioid overdoses in 2020 and 2021 were calculated 
using provisional 2020 populations data. Likewise, the number of fatal opioid overdoses were 
provisional at the time of report development. Other rates throughout are calculated using 
different denominators depending on the particular source of data (e.g., syndromic 
surveillance uses total number of events, hospital discharge uses total number of visits, AZ-
PIERS uses total number of responses). This report is cross-sectional analysis of events and 
rates for 2020-2021, with no additional statistical testing to quantify the significance of 
relationships between various factors and outcomes. As such data should be interpreted with 
caution in identifying potential associations, and without conclusions about cause and effect. 
 
Timeliness of Data 
Data for this report were pulled from June-August, 2022. All death data for 2021 were still 
considered provisional at this time. Several other data source data from 2021 may update 
slightly from what was reported as they become more complete. For this reason, data 
reported may differs slightly from what is reported elsewhere, and specifically on the ADHS 
Opioid Dashboard.  
 
Classification of Opioid Overdoses 
Case classification of opioid overdoses is complex and differs significantly between data 
sources. Most data and data systems (with the exception of MEDSIS for non-fatal overdoses) 
used for the surveillance of opioid overdoses were not created for the express purpose of 
capturing opioid overdoses. Estimates are likely a slight underrepresentation of the true 
prevalence of events in Arizona.  
 
Other Causes of Opioid Overdoses 
Most of this analysis could not consider social determinants of health of a particular opioid 
overdose, such as economic stability, access to health providers or behavioral health care, or 
other environmental impacts. These can be important contributors of opioid overdoses that 
are often overlooked.

<!-- page 258 -->
56 
 
Conclusions 
In 2020-2021, there were 3,888 opioid overdose deaths among Arizona residents. The leading 
cause was prescription and synthetic opioids (94.5%). The mortality rate for opioid overdose 
deaths was highest among males (39 per 100,000 persons), Black or African American (38 per 
100,000 persons), and persons 25-34 years old (59 per 100,000 residents) or 35-44 years old 
(49 per 100,000 persons). The overall trend of opioid overdose deaths increased significantly 
from 2017-2020, but was stable (not significantly increasing or decreasing) from 2020-2021. 
  
There were 7,448 reportable non-fatal opioid overdose events (MEDSIS), 18,203 suspected ED 
or inpatient visits (syndromic surveillance), and 104,450 ED or hospitalizations with any 
mention of opioids (hospital discharge records).  
 
Non-fatal opioid-related overdoses most commonly occurred among males (~65%), White 
(~58%) and Hispanic (~28%) persons, and persons ages 18-44 (~68%). The rate of non-fatal 
opioid-related overdoses were highest among males (67 per 100,000), American Indian or 
Alaska Native (35 per 100,000) and African American or Black persons (33 per 100,000), and 
persons ages 18-44 (289 per 100,000). The overall trend of non-fatal opioid overdoses was 
stable (not significantly increasing or decreasing) during 2020-2021.  
  
The number of opioid prescriptions, pills, and average morphine milligram equivalents (MME) 
dispensed in Arizona decreased during 2020-2021. 
 
There were 17,484 Emergency Medical Services (EMS)/Law Enforcement responses reported 
for suspected opioid overdoses. EMS/Law Enforcement response for suspected opioid 
overdoses occurred most commonly among males (66%), White (55%) and Hispanic (25%) 
persons, and persons ages 18-44 years old (62%).  
 
Naloxone was administered for 15,012 EMS/Law Enforcement responses for suspected opioid 
overdoses (>80% of all responses). Naloxone was administered most commonly by EMS 
(75%), Law Enforcement (19%), or a bystander (5%). Naloxone dispensed by pharmacies has 
continued to significantly increase during 2020-2021.  
  
Hospitalizations and ED visits for ED and hospitalizations with any mention of opioids 
resulted in approximately $2.2 billion in total charges annually in 2020-2021.

Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
White Mountain Lakes County Recreation Improvement District 
Implementation Update 
October 5, 2022  
General Effective Date: September 24, 2022 
Anticipated Timeline 
October 25, 2022: Public Hearing & Possible Resolution 
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors will hold a public hearing regarding a shift in the governance 
structure for the White Mountain Lakes County Recreation Improvement District (WMLCRID).  A 
resolution to establish the structure will be presented for Board consideration. 
October 26, 2022: Applications Available 
The WMLCRID Board Member Application Packets will be released via the Navajo County website and 
the White Mountain Lakes website.   
November 18, 2022:  Application deadline. 
Qualified electors wishing to serve on the WMLCRID must have their application submitted by this date 
in order to be considered. 
December, 2022: Applicant Review Process 
Applications are vetted and interviews with candidates are conducted. 
January, 2023: WMLCRID Board Member Appointments 
The Navajo County Supervisors votes to appoint the new WMLCRID Board of Directors. 
February/March, 2023: Board Member Training 
Navajo County staff coordinate with the WMCRID Board to schedule training initial training.

P a g e  | 1   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
 
C S A  P O L I C Y  G U I D E L I N E S 
Arizona’s county supervisors provide regional leadership over critical policy areas, including policies 
necessary to ensure safe communities, protect public health, promote economic development, and 
plan and manage lan d use for sustainable development.  Supervisors also provide important 
oversight of county operations, including overseeing strateg ies to promote sound fiscal 
management and to develop a high-quality county government workforce. 
 
For more than 30 years, CS A has served as a non -partisan forum for county officials to address 
important issues facing local constituents, providing a mechan ism to share information , and to 
develop a proactive state and federal policy agenda. 
 
CSA membership includes the 61 elected supervisors from Arizona’s 15 counties.  The association’s 
efforts are strengthened by the participation of top -level county profe ssional staff, who provide 
technical expertise necessary to inform policy decisions. 
 
All supervisors serve on the CSA Board of Directors and provide input into organizational strategy 
and the evaluation of operational performance.  The board annually elects six members to serve in 
CSA leadership positions.  Each county appoints a representative to the Leg islative Policy 
Committee (LPC).  The LPC meets as often as necessary to evaluate legislative measures and make 
policy recommendations to the full board. 
 
The LPC and CSA follow several policy guidelines: 
 
• Empower county boards of supervisors with sufficie nt authority to deal ef fectively with 
evolving and expanding local public needs and conditions.   
• Establish appropriate means to compensate c ounties for the cost of complying with state 
laws.   
• Provide sufficient fiscal capacity for counties to cope with cost increases, population growth, 
and escalating service demands.   
• Enable the counties to provide public services in a more responsive, efficient , and cost -
effective manner.   
• Define appropriate fiscal and administrative responsibilities within various state/county and 
municipal/county partnership programs.   
 
Though most business matters of the association require a simple majority vote to authorize action, 
legislative matters require a supermajority vote.

<!-- page 284 -->

P a g e  | 3   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
L E G I S L A T I V E  S U M M A R Y 
2022 SESSION NARRATIVE 
In the early morning of July 25, 2022, the second regular session Arizona’s 55th Legislature 
adjourned sine die, making September 24, 2022 , the general effective date. Over their 166 days in 
session, legislators introduced 1,851 bills and 152 memo rials and resolutions, seeing only 388 bills 
(20.9%) enacted and 16 (10.5%) of the memorials and resolutions passed. 
The state’s multi-billion-dollar budget surplus gave the Legislature capacity to address a variety of 
issues. Some members of the majority believed this surplus should be returned to taxpayers, while 
others thought it could fund long -sought priorities like water augmentation, debt redu ction, 
transportation infrastructure, and border security. This division in the majority caucus, along with 
the majority’s slim margins in both chambers, generated substantial disagreement on the session’s 
budget priorities.  
The Legislature’s path to a vi able FY 2023  budget enc ountered complications from dueling 
perspectives on state spending priorities among th e majority party, this was exemplified when the 
House Appropriations Committee attempted to pass a “skinny” budget in April.  This initial attempt 
to pass a baseline budg et with only statutorily required growth to continue critical government 
functions, while postponing spending the state’s large surplus until next session, was struck down in 
committee. The bills failed to gain support from any of the Democratic committee members and two 
Republican members voted to oppose the bill.  One of the Republican me mbers voted no because 
the budget spent too much money, the other because the budget did not spend enough on critical 
areas such as water, the border, and education. 
After months of debate, in the end, the budget was a bipartisan compromise between members  of 
the minority, majority leadership, and most of the majority party members resulting in a rare 
bipartisan budget.   
The $15.8 billion spending p lan for FY 2023 includes substantial investments across state 
government. Over the budget window from FY 2022 to FY 2025 the enacted budget adds             $5.4 
billion in one-time and $1.6 billion in new ongoing spending above Baseline levels. Some of the major 
funding items include over $1.0 billion for transportation, airport, and parks infrastructure, $1.0 
billion for w ater infrastructure, $1.2 billion for K -12 and higher education one -time and ongoing 
spending, $1.14 billion in state pension reduction, over $500 million for border security, $330 million 
to eliminate the state equalization property tax rate, $272 million for state salary adjustments, and 
$123 million in Arizona Health Care Cost Containment System ( AHCCCS) and Department of 
Economic Security (DES) provider rate increases.  
Elections 
In the 2022 session, the State Legislature maintained its post -2020 f ocus on passing measures 
intended to ensure the integrity and security of Arizona’s elections. During this year’s run-up to the 
2022 midterm elections, legislators introduced 140 elections -related bills.  Of these, only 15 bills 
became law.   
The introduce d bills ran the gamut of potential policy options, including modifying hand count 
requirements, increasing the automatic recount threshold, establishing a statewide voting holiday, 
mandating in-person voting, repealing the Active Early Voter List (AEVL), requiring hand tabulation 
of ballots, specifying that ballots include specific anti-fraud markings, and permitting the Legislature 
to “accept or reject the election results” after a completed canvass.

<!-- page 286 -->
P a g e  | 4   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
2022 SESSION NARRATIVE 
Despite support from  most members of the Legislature’s majority party, many of the session’s 
election bills were killed by members of the Senate majority that either disagr eed with their fellow 
members’ rationale for introducing the legislation or had concerns with the measure’s potential 
impact on election operations. One day in particular, March 14, saw 14 bills backed by the majority 
fail in a single Senate floor session.  One bill, SB 1008 elections; recount margin (Ugenti -Rita), saw 
strong bipartisan support, the bill increases the difference in votes necessary to trigger an automatic 
recount. Other successful efforts include SCR 1012 NOW: voter identification; affidavit; procedure 
(Mesnard) which submits to voters the question of whether the state should require additional 
information on their early ballot affidavits and will be on the November 2022 General Election ballot.  
Water 
A prolonged drought in the western United  States, combined with the ongoing impact of climate 
change and overallocation of the Colorado River, has reduced the river’s natural flows from          14.8 
million acre -feet (a/f) to 13.3 million  a/f over the past three decades. Efforts to manage these 
declining flows are widespread and ongoing, and Governor Ducey’s State of the State proposal to 
“secure Arizona’s water future” was his last opportunity to shape the state’s water policy in his final 
year in office. 
The Governor’s legislation progressed in fits and starts over the course of the session. While parties 
to the negotiations agreed on the scale of investment necessary to confront the state’s water issues, 
they disagreed about how the funds should be administered and allocated. The initial version of the 
legislation, previewed before a joint caucus in early-March by Speaker Bowers, would have created 
a new sta te agency – the Arizona Water Authority (AWA) - to administer funds intended for 
augmentation of the state’s water supplies over the long term and the Water Infrastructure Finance 
Authority’s (WIFA) Water Supply Development Fund. The Senate responded with their own 
solution, which empowered the state’s Water Infrastructure Finance Autho rity (WIFA) in place of 
the AWA and gave the Legislature a larger role in selecting the Authority’s board members.  
The compromise signed into law favored the Senate’s approach, by creating a new, bipartisan board 
for WIFA and establishing two new funds: t he Long-term Water Augmentation Fund (LTWAF) and 
the Water  Conservation Grant Fund (WCGF). The LTWAF will receive much of the $1 billion 
appropriation and is obligated to use  those funds to fund water supply development projects that 
import out-of-state water. The WCGF, a last-minute addition that – along with the board’s bipartisan 
structure - helped to secure Democratic votes, will reportedly receive $200 million to facilita te 
water conservation programs. 
Tax Revisions  
This session saw the Legislature’s Republican majority continue their focus on revising Arizo na’s 
taxes, with specific reductions coming to commercial property and business personal property taxes.  
The passage of SB 1093 equalization assistance; class one property (Mesnard) achieved the business 
community’s longtime goal of bringing the commercia l assessment ratio – or the percentage of a 
“Class 1” property’s value that is subject to tax - to 15 percent. This effor t began back in calendar 
year (CY) 2005 when th e assessment ratio was 25  percent. The commercial assessment ratio will 
phase down over two years, dropping from the current ratio – 16 percent - to 15.5 percent in CY 
2026 and 15 percent in CY 2027.

<!-- page 287 -->
P a g e  | 5   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
2022 SESSION NARRATIVE 
Another bill, HB 2822 personal property; additional depreciation (Weninger), would require county 
assessors to use a “ valuation factor” of 2.5  percent for specific classes of taxed business personal 
property classified during or after tax year (TY) 2022. Currently, these cl asses of business personal 
property have their assessed value depreciated on agency-determined schedules based on the year 
the property was acquired. The goal of the new structure is to provide business owners with an 
accelerated reduction of their tax bur den on property that decreases in value over ti me. Over the 
years, the Legislature has stayed focused on lowering a business’ tax burden connected to personal 
property. Representative Weninger’s bill would limit assessed value to 2.5 percent of the property’s 
depreciated value for business personal property c lassified in or after TY 2022. The property 
affected by this bill reflects 2.5 percent of the state’s total net assessed value.  
Salary Increases 
Members of the State Legislature, heeding calls for increased pay for sheriffs’ deputies, detention 
officers, and con stables, attempted early in the session  to remove local oversight from the salary 
determination process. 
In the case of SB 1371 county salaries; precinct officers (Pace), the Legislature considered removing 
the flexibility afforded to local boards of supervisors when determining constable salar ies. 
Currently, a board is permitted to fix constable salaries within several ranges set in statute 
delineated by a precinct’s number of registered voters. In more sparse ly populated areas, for 
example, where precinct s contain between 5,000 and 10,000 regi stered voters, the board is 
permitted to fix annual salaries for constables anywhere below $26,000. This legislation would have 
removed board oversight, fixing salaries for constables in those precincts at $45,000. Th is removal 
of local oversight was duplicated in every precinct size. Ultimately, the bill did not pass in the 2022 
session.  
The Governor’s FY 2023 Executive Budget, outlined a series of salary increases for state employees, 
judges, and for the Department of  Public Safety (DPS).  SB 2541 deputy  sheriff; detention officer; 
salary (Payne) originated due to the upward pressure the statewide increase would have on 
recruitment and retention of public safety employees in counties, since they pull from the same 
talent pool.  The legislation would have tied the starting salaries of specified officers to the “average 
starting salary of the two highest paying law enforcement agencies, which included DEPS. The 
County Supervisors Association (CSA) opposed this legislation, noting that it failed to contemplat e 
counties’ ongoing efforts to compete in the space, stripped authorities from boards of supervisors, 
and failed to provide state funding to cove r the costs.  Due to serious concerns over the impact the 
bill would have on counties CSA, the Arizona Sheriffs  Association, and the Arizona Association of 
Counties agreed to pursue a one-time $53.4 million appropriation to county sheriffs in FY 2023 for 
the provision of recruitment and retention bonus es to their line -level deputies and detention 
officers.

<!-- page 288 -->
P a g e  | 6   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
2022 CSA LEGISLATIVE AGENDA 
CSA LEGISLATIVE AGENDA  
HB 2012: county improvement districts; formation (Kavanagh) Specifies that a petition addressed 
to the county board of supervisors requesting the establishment of an improvement district must be 
signed by both a majority of the persons owning real property and by the owners of 51 percent or  
more of the real proper ty within the limits of the proposed district. Prior to this law becoming 
effective, these petitions could contain either of the requirements.  Laws 2022, Chapter 92 
HB 2044: juven ile dependency; state aid; appropriation  (Biasiucci) Establishes the State Aid for 
Juvenile Dependency Proceedings Fund (SAJDP Fund), administered by the Arizona Crim inal 
Justice Commission, to provide state aid to county public defenders, legal defenders , and contract 
indigent defense counsel for the processing of juvenile dependency cases. Requires the Commission 
to distribute monies in the State Aid to Indigent Defen se Fund – on or before September 1 of each 
fiscal year (FY) - to each county in which the three -year average of th e total juvenile dependency 
case filings  in the county’s superior court exceeds the three -year average of similar filings in the 
county’s superior court for FY 2013 through FY 2015 based on each county’s proportional share of 
the increase in petitions. Requires each county board of supervisors to separately account for these 
monies and specifies that the monies must be spent to provide state aid to county public defenders, 
legal defenders, and contract indigent defense counsel for the processing of juvenile  dependency 
cases. Appropriates $2 millio n from the general fund in FY 2023 to the SAJDP Fund.  Added to the 
budget on going (Laws 2022, Chapter 311, Sec. 7). 
HB 2067: county improvement districts; recreation; governance (Blackman) Specifies in session law 
that the board of supervisors of a county with a population of less than 125,000 persons and a county 
improvement district providing recreational improvements is authorized to transition the district’s 
governance to transition to a separately elected three-member or five-member board of directors. 
If exercised, initial directors would be appointed by the board of s upervisors, and subsequent 
directors must be elected by the district’s qualified voters. The board of directors has the powers 
granted to a dist rict pursuant to statute. Contains a delayed repeal date of January 1, 2026.  Laws 
2022, Chapter 93 
HB 2197: medical examiner; pathologist assistant; autopsies  (Osborne) Permits a county medical 
examiner or alternate medical examiner to authorize "pathologist assistants" (defined) to assist with 
performing autopsies under the direct supervisio n of a licensed physicia n who is board certified in 
forensic pathology. Specifies that a  pathologist assistant cannot certify a cause of death or perform 
an autopsy independently.  Laws 2022, Chapter 82 
SB 1188: unincorporated areas; transient lodging assessment  (Gabaldon) Permits the board of 
supervisors of a county with a population of less than 500,000 persons to levy an assessment on the 
gross proceeds of sales - or gross income - of county businesses providing transient lodging. Specifies 
the assessment must be levied on Janu ary or July 1 depending on which date occurs at least three 
months after the county’s approval of the assess ment. Limits the assessment rate to a maximum of 
six percent. Further specifies the tax applies only in the county’s unincorporated areas and that the 
county is required to use revenues from the tax to promote and enhance tourism and for "economic 
development activities" (defined elsewhere in statute).  Held in Senate.

CSA FINANCIAL AGENDA 
HB 2201: counties; committed youth contributions; repeal (Weninger) Repeals statute requiring the 
Department of Juvenile Corrections to annually assess a committed youth confinement cost sharing 
fee to counties with more than 500,000 residents (Maricopa County and Pima County) and deposit 
the revenues into the Department of  Juvenile Corrections Local Cost Sharing Fund. Specifies that, 
when effective, all unexpended and unencumbered monies remaining in the Fund are transferred to 
the general fund and further passed  to the Department of Administration for proportional 
distribution to each county using population data from the most recent U.S. decennial census.  Held 
in Senate.

COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
HB 2119: civil rights restoration; requirements; process  (Kaiser) States that individuals who 
complete probation for an offense committed outside the state’s jurisdiction, or who have received 
absolute discharge from a non -Arizona state or federal pris on, is eligible for automatic restoration 
of civil rights on full pa yment of required restitution. Outlines notification r equirements for 
individuals about the potential restoration of their rights by the courts and probation departments. 
Requires the clerk of the court to notify the Department of Public Safety when a person’s civil rights 
are restored and include whether the right to possess a firearm has been restored . Laws 2022, 
Chapter 199 
HB 2159: law enforcement officers; polygraph; examinations  (Kavanagh) Law enforcement 
employers are no longer allowed to require a law enforcement officer to submit to a polygraph 
examination in certain circumstances. During disciplinary action of a law enforcement officer, the 
hearing officer, administrative law judge, or appeals board are permitted to take into consideration 
violations of statutes regulating peace officer discipline as mitigation in determining discipline. 
During an investigation involving multiple law enforcement officers, if evidence is discovered that 
exonerates a law enforcement officer, the employer is required to issue the individual law 
enforcement officer a notice of findings. Laws 2022, Chapter 175 
HB 2251: NOW: lasers; peace officers; aircraft; penalty  (Finchem) Makes changes to the penalty 
aiming a laser pointer at a peace officer or an occupied aircraft , and modifies the definition of l aser 
pointer. Laws 2022, Chapter 260 
HB 2253: NOW: threshold amount; fe ntanyl (Grantham) Establishes a statutory drug “threshold 
amount” of nine grams for fentanyl or fentanyl mimetic substances. Laws 2022, Chapter 371 
HB 2254: officers bill of rights; preemption  (Grantham) States that the peace officers’ bill of rights 
does not preempt agreements that supplement or enhance its provisions. Prior to this bill becoming 
effective, statute stated that the peace officers’ bill of rights did not preempt agreements which 
“supplant, revise, or otherwise deviate” from its provisions. Laws 2022, Chapter 88 
HB 2284: NOW: search; rescue; human remains; canines (Fillmore) Allows a search and rescue unit 
established or recognized by a county to possess human remains for the purpose of training canines 
for search and rescue work. The sheriff is required to keep an inventory of all human remains that  
are kept for the purpose of training search and rescue canines. Expands when an anatomical gift may 
be made, to include a search a rescue unit established or recognized by any federal, state, county, or 
local government entity to train search and rescue canines. Laws 2022, Chapter 374 
HB 2309: detained juveniles; advisements; n otifications (Jermaine) A peace officer who ta kes a 
juvenile into temporary custody is required to advise the juvenile before questioning of the juvenile's 
Miranda rights in language that i s comprehensible to a juvenile and, as soon as is practicable, is 
required to make a good f aith effort to notify the juvenile's parents, guardian or custodian of the 
juvenile's custody, unless doing so would pose a risk to the juvenile. The peace officer is also required 
to advise the juvenile's parents, guardian or custodian of the juvenile's Miranda rights. If a juvenile is 
a ward of the state, the peace officer is required to notify the public defender, any guardian ad litem, 
or a court-appointed special advocate of the juvenile's custody. Laws 2022, Chapter 375

<!-- page 291 -->
P a g e  | 9   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
HB 2319: law enforcement activity; recording prohibition  (Kavanagh) Makes it a Class 3 
misdemeanor to knowingly make a video r ecording of "law enfo rcement activity" within eight feet 
of where the law enforcement activity is occurring, if the person has received a warning from a law 
enforcement officer that they are prohibited from recording.  Allows a person to record within eight 
feet if a law enfor cement officer gives permission or the law enforcement activity occurs in an 
enclosed structure and the recording is not interfering. Laws 2022, Chapter 376 
HB 232 4: animal fighting; cockfighting; minors presence  (Kavanagh) States that a  person who 
knowingly causes, allows, or assists a minor to attend an animal fight or cockfight, or in a location  
where animal fighting or cockfighting preparations are being made, is guilty of a Class 1 
misdemeanor. Emergency clause. Laws 2022, Chapter 138 
HB 2340: disciplinary appeals; final disposition reporting (Payne) Requires the presiding authority 
to issue a final de cision i mmediately after the conclusion  of disciplinary action  involving a law 
enforcement official, and provide any amended findings to the enforcement agency that initiated the 
discipline. Directs the agency to include the final disposition in its original investigation record and 
forward the document to any prosecuting agency provided with information during t he 
investigation. Laws 2022, Chapter 139 
HB 2343: crime scene investigation  interference; offense  (Payne) Specifies t hat if a person 
knowingly disobeys a peace officer’s reasonable verbal order to remain off the premises of a possible 
crime scene or otherwise inte rferes with an investigation is considered a Class 2 misdemeanor for 
“interfering with a crime scene investigation.”  Clarifies that an individual may peaceably observe a 
law enforcement investigation of a crime scene. Laws 2022, Chapter 262 
HB 2347: law enforcement; misconduct investigations ; extension  (Payne) A law enforcement 
employer is allowed to continue an investigation of employee misconduct beyond the 180-calendar-
day period only if it is demonstrated that addit ional time is necessary to obtain or review evidence. 
Requires the employer to provide the employee with a written explanation of any reasons for t he 
extension prior to exceeding the 180-calendar-day limit.   Prohibits an extension from exceeding an 
additional 180 calendar days  and requires the matt er to be dismissed if the investigation is not 
complete at the conclusion of the extension period . Allows the employer to initiate a new 
investigation of the employee for misconduct if there is  newly discovered m aterial evidence that 
could not with reasonable diligence have been discovered during the initial 180 -calendar-day limit 
or extension. Laws 2022, Chapter 378 
HB 2349: peace officer standards board; membership (Payne) Requires the two law enforcement 
officers “who have knowledge of and experience in representing peace officers in disciplinary cases”  
on the  Arizona Peace Officer S tandards and Training Board (AZPOST)  to be from different law 
enforcement agencies and may not serve in a supervisory capacity. Laws 2022, Chapter 153 
HB 2355: second degree murder; se ntencing (Biasiucci) Increases the sentencing guidelines for a 
person tried as an adult or at least 18 years of age who is convicted of a dangerous crime against 
children (1 st degree) involving murder of a minor who is under 15 years of age (2 nd degree) to a  
minimum (25 years), presumptive (30 years), and maximum (35 years) , if a life sentence is n ot 
imposed. Laws 2022, Chapter 245 
HB 25 83: NOW: DUI; data collection; study committee  (Kavanagh) Establishes a 14 -member 
Driving Under the Influence (DUI) Data Collection Study Committee to gather information on the 
current DUI-related data collection requirements and to make recommendations how to establish a 
centralized DUI-related data collection and reporting. The Committee is required to submit a report 
of its findings and recommendations to the Governor and the Legislature by December 16, 2022 .  
Contains a delayed repeal date of October 1, 2023. Laws 2022, Chapter 206

<!-- page 292 -->
P a g e  | 10   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
HB 2604: NOW: emergency orders of protection; sup erior court  (Bolick) Extends an order of 
protection’s length to two year s, from one year. Extends the length of an emergency order of 
protection to one week, from 72 hours. In counties with a population of more than 150,000 persons, 
and during hours of court closure, requires the superior court’s presiding judge to make a judg e, 
justice of the peace, magistrate, or commissioner available to issue emergency orders of protection 
by telephone. Laws 2022, Chapter 167 
HB 2609: NOW: ambulance services; certificat es of necessity  (Burges) Starting January 1, 2024, 
makes v arious changes to the application and issuance of certificates of nec essity for ambulance 
service starting January 1, 2024.  Requires the Department of Health Services (DHS) is required to 
review and determine if ambulance response times need to be updated every six years if the 
population of the service area changes by at least 10 percent, while considering the impact a change 
would have on the rates.   Specifies that ambulance services must install and maintain an electronic 
global positioning system monitoring device in each vehicle to record response time, but allows for 
a waiver for  ambulance service s that can reasonably demon strate they are unable  to meet th e 
requirement. When dispatch or global positioning system connectivity is not available, an ambulance 
service is required to manually document the on -scene arrival times for response time  
measurement. DHS is required to notify all interested parties, including municipalities, fire districts, 
and existing certificate of necessity holders within the service area, of any applications for an initial 
or amended certificate of necessity within 15 days after an application is filed, within 15 days after 
the application is complete, and w ithin 15 days after a decision on the application by DH S. Any 
certificate of necessity holder whose ambulance service area is within the affected service area of a 
new ambulance service is allowed to appeal a DHS determination within 30 days after the decision. 
If an appeal is made, DHS must hold a public hearing and notify every interested party of the hearing. 
DHS must require a public hearing on any proposed action relating to an adjustment of general public 
rates, charges, or certificate of necessity transfers, with some exceptions.          Laws 2022, Chapter 
381  
HB 2664: NOW: military and transitional housing fund; monies (Payne) Establishes the Military and 
Transitional Housing Fund, to be administered by the Arizona Department of Housing (ADOH). 
ADOH is required to spend Fund monies for developing projects an d programs to provide 
transitional housing opportunities for military members who will be separating or who have recently 
separated from the military to be housed in newly constructed housing facilities. Specifies that 
monies cannot be spent on unoccupied hotels and motels designated for homeless veterans. 
Requires ADOH to report to the Legislature by September 1 of each year on the status of the Fund. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 382 
HB 2675: NOW: anti-semitism; criminal offenses; data collection  (Biasiucci) Requires the 
Department of Public Safety (DPS) to collect information concerning criminal offenses that manifest 
evidence of prejudice based on "anti-Semitism" and requires justice agencies of the state or political 
subdivisions to provide DPS with information concerning these crimes. Laws 2022, Chapter 186 
HB 2695: forfeiture; substitute assets; postdeprivation hearing (Grantham) Authorizes the court to 
order a person to forfeit "substitute assets" after being convicted of an offense for which forfeiture 
applies. Specifies the court is no longer required to grant a claimant's motion to release the forfeited 
property due if the vi ctim of the alleged crime that gave rise to the forfeiture files a claim to seized 
property.  Requirements surrounding the return of property in postdeprivation does not prevent the 
state from returning any property to the property owner or prevent the filing of a racketeering lien 
or a restitution lien. Laws 2022, Chapter 131

<!-- page 293 -->
P a g e  | 11   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
HB 2696: mandatory sentenc es; children; trafficking; smuggling  (Biasiucci) Establishes new 
minimum, presumptive, and maximum sentences for individuals convicted of a dangerous crime 
against children in the f irst degree involving continuous sexual abu se or previously convicted of a 
predicate felony. Specifies that a variety of offenses, including trafficking for forced labor or services, 
sexual extortion, and participating in a human smuggling operation are no t eligible for sentence 
suspension, probation, pardon, or release except in specific circumstances. Laws 2022, Chapter 197 
HB 2709: v ictim’s right to privacy; exception  (Blackman) Specifies that the  requirement for a law 
enforcement agency or prosecution agency to redact a victim's identifying information from records 
pertaining to a criminal case does not apply to the victim's add ress if the address appears in any 
body-worn camera footage, photographs, or other visual or audio depictions and there is evidence 
that the defendant knows the victim's address because of specified relationships (if the victim and 
defendant are married, w ere married, or share  a child). States a court can order the victim's 
identifying and locating information to be disclosed in a specific case if it is necessary to protect the 
defendant's constitution al rights or when the information is not reasonably able  to be redacted . 
Places limits on how the defendant’s attorney can communicate this information and permits the 
victim to be heard by the court prior to the disclosure. Laws 2022, Chapter 100 
HB 2721: law enforcement officers; AZPOST (Kavanagh) Retroactive to January 1, 2021, specifies 
that the members hip of any government entity that investigates law enforcement officer 
misconduct mus t consist of at least  two-thirds Arizona Peace Officer Standards and Training 
(AZPOST) Board certified law enforcement officers.  Requires a m ajority vote to initiate an 
investigation of law enforcement officer misconduct, influence the conduct of or certify misconduct 
investigations, recommend  disciplinary action , or impose discipline for law enforcement officer 
misconduct. E xempts the membership and voting requirements for the AZPOST Board, an entity 
that only reviews certain actions, an entity that investigates federal employment discrimination, or 
a multijurisdictional law enforcement agency or task force that is charged with investigating the use 
of force by law enforcement officers, if at least two-thirds of the membership consist s of AZPOST 
Board certified law enforcement officers. Laws 2022, Chapter 386 
HB 2722: strategic  actions; public participation  (Toma) Expands this abil ity to dismiss or quash a 
strategic action againts public participation.  Requires the person who files a motion to dismiss or 
quash an action to establish proof that the legal action was motivated by a desire to deter or prevent 
the lawful exercise of a constitutional right.  The court is required to grant a motion to dismiss unless 
the state actor can show that legal action was justified, and the action was not motivated to prevent 
a constitutional right.  Constraints a severability clause. Laws 2022, Chapter 267 
HB 2723: civil case assignment; judges (Toma) Requires, in counties with a population of more than 
one million persons, that t he assignment of a civil case to a superior court judge be done by either 
automated means or a formula that is approved by the Supreme C ourt.  I f the assigned judge 
becomes unavailable, the superior court may take judicial availability into account when reassigning 
the case or to assign a case to the appropriate specialty court.  Prohibits a judge from selecting a case 
for assignment or refusing to accept an assigned case except for reasons of good cause, a court rule, 
or ethical considerations. Laws 2022, Chapter 283 
SB 1060: survival of action; deceased sheriff  (Borrelli) Repeals statute allowing any action for the 
malfeasance, misfeasance, or nonfeasance of a sheriff or sheriff deputies to be prosecuted against 
the personal representative of the sheriff or sheriff deputies. Laws 2022, Chapter 27

<!-- page 294 -->
P a g e  | 12   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
SB 1069: juvenile dependency; child placement  (Barto) Makes various changes to statute relating 
to juvenile dependency  guardianship and adoption hearings . Requires the cou rt to appoint an 
attorney for a child in delinquency proceedings that commence with a petition or that may involve 
detention, instead of in all delinquency proceedings. Permits admission of “out-of-court statements” 
or nonverbal conduct by the child regard ing acts of abuse or neglect in guardianship proceedings. 
Allows the court to establish permanent guardianship if the dependency petition is filed by a person 
other than the Department of Child Safety (DCS), and notice has been provided to all parties and no 
party objects.  Specifies that contested termination adjudication hearings are not subject to 
requirements that proceedings be open to the public.  Allows DCS to object to a motion and takes 
further action when a non -DCS dependency petition is filed to protect the health and safety of the 
child including moving to intervene in the dependency action or filing a separate petition .   Laws 
2022, Chapter 5 
SB 1073: juveniles; adjudication; disposition; probation (Barto) Makes changes to juvenile intensive 
probation and wh en a juvenile being charged as an adult may be transferred to an adult facility.  
Removes the prohibition on housing juveniles charged with a dangerous offense from being held in 
a juvenile detention facility.  Requires the court to consider the juveniles age, maturity level, mental 
state, circumstances of the alleged offense, history, and ability of the available facilities to meet the 
needs of the juvenile before when determining whether a juvenile charg ed with specified felony 
offenses should be detained in a juvenile detention center or an adult facility.  Allows the director of 
Juvenile Court Services in the county where the juvenile is held to request a transfer to an adult 
facility based on conduct.  Requires the juvenile intensive probation team to m aintain contact with 
the juvenile’s school, employer, restitution agency or treatment program as deeme d appropriate, 
instead of maintaining weekly contact.  Adds a juvenile probation officer to the list o f persons that 
may be part of a juvenile intensive p robation team, and a one -person juvenile intensive probation 
team may supervise up to 15 juveniles at a time. Allows juvenile probation officers to modify a 
juvenile’s level of intensive probation and activity requirements. Removes the ability of the intensive 
probation team to request the county attorney to bring a noncompliant probationer or a probationer 
who fails to pay restitution or probation fees before the court. Specifies monies in the Juvenile 
Probation Services fund may be used to obtain, operate,  and maintain a state -approved case 
management system for individuals on probation or referred to juvenile court. Requires the court to 
appoint an attorney for a child in delinquency proceedings that comm ence with a petition or that 
may involve detention, instead of in all delinquency proceedings. Laws 2022, Chapter 6 
SB 1075: incompetent defendant; guardian appointment; costs  (Barto) When a court finds that a 
defendant is incompetent to stand trial and will not regain competency within 21 months, allows any 
party to request the court appoint a guardian ad litem to investigate whether the defendant is, or may 
be, in need of a guardian, a conservator or any other protective order. Specifies that the guardian ad 
litem who determines that protective action is appropriate must initiate proceedings for the 
appointment of a guardian, a conservator, or any other protective order. Permits a court, in response 
to these proceedings, to order an independent evaluation of the defend ant. Specifies that guardian 
ad litem is entitled to reasonable compensation for the work performed and that a county will be 
responsible for the compensation if the defendant is indigent. Laws 2022, Chapter 158

<!-- page 295 -->
P a g e  | 13   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
 
SB 1079: child placement; procedural time limits (Barto) Specifies, with respect to child placement, 
that procedural tim e limits for the Department of Child Safety cannot be waived, extended, or 
continued unless it is necessary for the full, fair, and proper presentati on of evidence. States that a 
continuance of a procedural time limit cannot be granted if the delay is not in the best interest of the 
child, and that a continuance beyond 30 days ca n be granted only on a finding of extraordinary 
circumstances. Prohibits t he court from extending procedural time limits for more than 60 days 
unless the court makes a written findi ng that substantial evidence exists that the additional time is 
in the best  interest of the child. Permits the court to impose sanctions if it determ ines that an 
extension was due to a party's disclosure violation or lack of due diligence. In cases where a petition 
for terminating a parent -child relationship is contested, require s the court to hold a termination 
adjudication hearing within 90 days of the initial severance hearing.  Similarly, requires the court to 
hold the guardianship adjudication hearing within 90 days of the initial guardianship hearing.  Laws 
2022, Chapter 30 
SB 1114: court -ordered treatment; case records; confidentiality  (Barto) Modifies the emergency 
admissions process to include a person who has a persistent or acute disability a nd is unable or 
unwilling to undergo voluntary evaluation.  Specifies that in voluntary treatment hearings , the case 
records and information are not open to the public , but allows the courts to adopt rules to govern 
the records and release the case informati on for good cause.  Allows the applicant to include 
statements from other people who witnessed behavior that would indicate the person is a danger to 
self or others , and removes the r equirement that the applicant for emergency admission for 
evaluation have personal observation. Requires in a petition for court -ordered treatment where 
there is an existing guardian to include a statement identifying the guardian, and a request that the 
court consider imposing additional duties upon them. Repeals the requiremen t that a petition for 
court-ordered treatment involving court action be sent to the public fiduciary of the patient’s county 
of residence, instead requiring that such petitions be ma iled to any person or agency that is 
nominated as a guardian or conservator.  Laws 2022, Chapter 299 
SB 1210: ment ally ill; transportation; evaluation; treatment  (Barto) Specifies, if a municipality or 
county contracts with an authorized transporter, the court, an admitting officer, mental health  
treatment agency or evaluation agency to authorize apprehension and transportation of a proposed 
patient to an evaluation agency by an authorized transporter when there are reasonable grounds to 
believe the patient or proposed patient may be safely transp orted without a peace officer.  Defines 
authorized transporter as a transportation entity that is contracted with a city, town, or county to 
provide authorized transportation and is either an ambulance company that holds a valid certificate 
of necessity or  transportation provider authorized by the state to provid e safe behavioral health 
transportation. Establishes the Alternative Behavioral Health Transportation Study Committee 
(Committee) to make recommendations on the requirements for establishing behavio ral health 
transportation providers and to submit a report by December 31, 2024.  The Committee is repealed 
on July 1, 2024.  Allows a cit y or county to use process servers instead of peace officers to serve a 
person ordered to undergo involuntary evaluation with a copy of the petitions, affidavit, and notice 
of hearing. Laws 2022, Chapter 250

<!-- page 296 -->
P a g e  | 14   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
SB 1251: armed robbery; deadly weapon; classifica tion (Gowan) Expands the list of actions that 
constitute armed robbery, to include if the person or an accomplice takes possession of or attempts 
to take possession of a deadly weapon during a robbery. Laws 2022, Chapter 348 
SB 1310: NOW: incompetent defendants; dangerous; involuntary commitment  (Barto) Effective 
January 1, 2024, a new process to allow any party to request the court order a trial to determine if a 
defendant is dangerous and should be involuntarily committed is established.  A court may order a 
trial to determine if the defendan t is dangerous and remand the defendant for civil com mitment 
procedures and must hold a hearing to determine if there is proof the defe ndant committed the 
serious offense within 10 days.   If the court does not find sufficient proof, then either the defendant 
is ordered to treatment that does not involve involuntary commitment or released from custody and 
the charges dropped.   If the cou rt does find there is sufficient proof the defendant committed the 
serious acts, then the court must hold a trial within 120 days to determine dangerousness.  Allow s 
both the state and the defendant to retain a mental health expert to examine the defe ndant and 
present on the defendant’s mental health evaluation at the dangerousness trial.  Requires 
competency evaluation repo rts to include if the defendant should be c onsidered dangerous, 
information on what makes the defendant likely to be dangerous, and if the defendant has a history 
of dangerous conduct.  Specifies a court appointed mental health expert must submit a report within 
ten working days of completing an examination and provides report requirements.   If the defendant 
is not found to be danger ous for involuntary commitment, then the court must either remand the 
defendant to an evaluation agency for civil commitment proceedings, appoint a guardian, or dismiss 
the charges and release the defendant from custody.   Outlines a process for the defendant to receive 
a biannual examination to determine if the defendant remains dangerous or has been restored to 
competency and outlines the hearing process if the defendant is found to no longer be dangerous or 
incompetent to stand trial. Laws 2022, Chapter 352  
SB 1566: state bar; attorney charges; damages (Leach) If an attorney who is the subject of the charge 
prevails in an attorney discipline matter, the State Bar of Arizona is responsible to the attorney for 
any attorney fees and court costs. Laws 2022, Chapter 226 
SB 1593: sexual offenses; forensic examination; e xpenses (Kerr) Requires counties to pay for 
examinations needed to secure evidence that a person is either a victim of a dangerous crime against 
children or a specified sexual offense.  Prohibits health care institutions and health care providers 
from directly or indirectly issuing additional balance bills for th ese medical interviews or forensi c 
examinations. Prohibits health care institutions from charging intake, consultation, facility, or other 
fees to patients in these circumstances. Requires counties to publish the name of the county official 
who is responsible for paying the expenses on their website. Laws 2022, Chapter 117 
SB 1607: state guard; volunteers  (Townsend) Compels the Governor to prom ulgate rules for the 
Arizona State Guard that establish a chain of command and create a process by which individuals 
may volunteer for service and report for duty. Laws 2022, Chapter 222 
SB 1624: unauthorized disclosure; intimate images; remedies  (Mesnard) Specifies that a person is 
civilly liable for intentionally disclosing, or threatening to disclose, an intimate image of ano ther 
person if the individual depicted suffers harm from the disclosure. Specifies that the individual who 
disclosed the image must know that a) the depicted individual did not consent, b) the intimate image 
was private, and c) the depicted ind ividual was identifiable. Provides various exemptions, which 
include disclosures made in good faith to law enforcement, in a legal p roceeding, or for medical 
education and treatment, among other scenarios. Specifies what the plaintiff can recover and how 
their identity can be protected by the court during proceedings. Laws 2022, Chapter 118

<!-- page 297 -->
P a g e  | 15   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
COURTS, CRIMINAL JUSTICE, AND PUBLIC SAFETY 
SB 1633: harassment; aggravated harassment; offense (Kerr) Expands the statutory list of acts that 
constitute aggravated  harassment to include any other criminal -related injunction against the 
individual on behalf of the victim, an emergency or der of protection, and a violation of a person’s 
court-ordered release condition of no contact with the victim. Further specifies th at previous 
convictions of offenses domestic violence enumerated in A.R.S. § 13 -3601 may only be considered 
for the purpose of determining aggravated harassment if the offenses were committed against the 
victim. Exempts professional investigators and peace  officers working withi n the scope of their 
duties and certified/duly authorized process servers from the definition of hara ssment.                          
Laws 2022, Chapter 291 
SB 1653: lifetime injunction; crime victim (Kerr) Establishes proceedings for a lifetime injunction 
that prohibits a defendant from contacting a victim during the defendant’s natural lifetime. Requires 
the court, at the time of sentencing and on the request of the victim or prosecutor, to issue a lifetime 
injunction if the defendant  is convicted of specified offenses. Requires the  court to provide the 
Department of Public Safety (DPS) with injunction information  for later inclusion in the National 
Crime Information System and that permit victims to request  an injunction for a defenda nt 
sentenced prior to this law’s effective date. Outlines the three scenarios whereby the injunction can 
be lifted: a) if the defend ant shows the court that the victim has died, b) if the conviction has been 
dismissed, expunged,  or overturned, or if the de fendant has been pardoned, and c) if the victim 
submits a request for the injunction’s early expiration. Laws 2022, Chapter 278

EDUCATION 
EDUCATION 
HB 2124: NOW: school districts; tuition; expenditures  (Udall) Starting July 1, 2023 , high sc hool 
pupils residing in a common school district not within a high school district are considered enrolled 
in the school district they attend.  Requires a county board of supervisors to levy an additional tax in 
specified school districts – common districts not within a high school district  – equal to the 
countywide average per pupil equalization base for high school pupils multiplied by the number of 
resident high school pupils in the common school district not within a high school district during the 
prior year. Specifies this money shall be added to the County Aid fo r Equalization Assistance for 
Education. Directs  the Departm ent of Education  (Department) to provide the counties with the 
information necessary to make these calculations. Repeals the ability of district governing boards to 
budget for the bond issues port ion of the cost of tuition charged to the district for pupil s attending 
school out -of-district. Directs the Arizona Department to accept and review requests for grants 
from the “Extraordinary Special Education Needs fund” from school districts offering high school 
services. Specifies applicants must demonstrate a s ubstantial and negative financial impact 
associated with accepting students with special education needs who previous ly had been paid 
through tuition and now are accepted via open enrollment.  Allows a high school in a unified district 
to charge tuition to a “transporting district,” or a district that does not offer classes in the necessary 
grades if the transporting district student does not present a certificate of educational convenience. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 285 
HB 2161: parental rights; schools; education records  (Kaiser) States that this  state, a political 
subdivision of this state, or any other governmental entity or official “shall not interfere with or usurp 
the fundamental right of parents to direct the upbringing, education, health care, and mental health 
of their children .” Permits parents to bring suit against entities and officials based on violations of 
these rig hts. States that, on suit, an entity or official must demonstrate the “interference or 
usurpation” is narrowly tailored and accomplishes a compelling government interest to continue the 
action in question. States that parents have “the right to access all written and electronic records of 
a school district or school district employee concerning the parent’s child.” Requires schools to 
obtain written consent from parents before administering surveys to students. Laws 2022, Chapter 
200 
HB 2179: schools; accounting responsibility plans; counties  (Udall) Requires school districts 
applying to the State Board of Education t o assume accounting responsibility to notify and provide 
the county treasurer and county school superintendent with a copy of its plan to assume  
responsibility. Laws 2022, Chapter 192 
HB 2325: schools; instruction; 9/11 education day  (Kavanagh) Establishes September 11 in each 
year as “9/11 Education Day,” but is not a legal holiday.  Requires on “9/11 Education Day,” that each 
public school in this state shall dedicate a portion of the school day to age-appropriate education on 
the terrorist attacks of September 11, 2001. Directs the State Board of Education (SBE) to develop 
a list of age -appropriate resources aligning with ac ademic standards relating to the September 11, 
2001, terrorist attacks. Additionally, directs the SBE to establi sh a process for public schools to 
recommend resources. Laws 2022, Chapter 244
