EDUCATION 
HB 2439: school library books; parental review  (Pingerelli) Requires school district governing 
boards and charter school governing bodies to establish, by January 1, 2023, procedures to allow 
parents access t o t he collection of books and materials available in the school library  and allow 
parents to access lists of books and materials their children borrowed from the library.  Additionally, 
public and charter school administration s are required to digitally publish lists of purchased books 
and materials for public review for at least 60 days af ter the purchase.  Exempts district schools or 
charter schools without a full-time library media specialist or schools that have an agreement with a 
county free library district or municipal library. Laws 2022, Chapter 205 
HB 2853: Arizona empowerment scholarship accounts; appropriation  (Toma) Beginning in the 
2022-23 school year, any Arizona resident who is eligible t o enroll in a public school in Arizona , a 
preschool program for children with disabilities, a kindergarten program, or any of grades 1 through 
12 is eligible for an Arizona Empowerment Scholarship Account (ESA). Expands how the monies in a 
qualified student's ESA account may be used to include public transportation services between the 
student's residence and a qualified school, and computer hardware  and te chnological devices  
primarily used for an educational purpose. Cuts in half the number of logged hours of instruction a 
qualified student who is enrolled in Arizona online instruction must receive in order to be eligible for 
an ESA. App ropriates $2.2 million and 26 FTE positions from t he state general fund in FY 2023 to 
the Department of Education to administer ESA accounts. Retroactively effective as of July 1, 2022. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 388

<!-- page 300 -->
P a g e  | 18   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
ELECTIONS 
ELECTIONS 
HB 2236: voter registration; request required  (Hoffman) Specifies that all state agencies, 
departments, divisions, and political subdivision  are prohibited from registering a person to vote 
without the person’s affirmative request. Laws 2022, Chapter 259 
HB 2237: same day voter registration; prohibition (Hoffman) Prohibits same day voter registration. 
Specifies it is a Class 6 felony to register a person on election day and deem the person eligible to 
vote in that election. Provides exemptions for qualified persons covered under the Uniformed and 
Overseas Citizens Absentee Voting Act who are temporarily absent from the state.                                              
Laws 2022, Chapter 252 
HB 224 3: voter registration; state residency; cancellation  (Hoffman) Requires t he voter 
registration form to contain a statement  notifying the registrant that if they permanently move to 
another state after registering to vote in Arizona, the registrant's voter registration will be canceled. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 370 
HB 2492: voter registration; verification; citizenship  (Hoffman) Requires Arizona residents to 
provide satisfactory evidence of United States citizenship including proof of location of residence, 
date and place of birth, and a mark in the "yes" box next  to the question regarding citizenship prior 
to being registered to vote.  Requires the county recorder or other officer in charge of elections to 
send a notice to the applicant if the app lication for registration lacks satisfactory evidence of 
citizenship.  Establishes a list of entities the county recorder or other officer in charge of elections is 
required to compare  against the information available on the application for registration i f the 
county has access.  Directs the county recorder to reject any application for registration that is not 
accompanied by satisfactory evidence of citizenship and states that a knowing failure to reject such 
application for registration is a Class 6 felony. Requires a county recorder cancel a voter registration 
when the county recorder receives and confirms information that the registered person is not a 
United States citizen. Directs the Attorney General to submit a report to the Secretary of State and 
the Legislature detail ing all findings relating to the citizenship st atus of individuals who are 
registered to vote and who have not provided satisfactory evidence of citizenship. Contains a 
severability clause . Modified by Laws 2022, Chapter 174 , to in clude a delayed effective date of  
January 1, 2023.  Laws 2022, Chapter 99 
HB 2703: NOW: secure online signature collection  (Bolick) Directs the Secretary of State (SOS) to 
continue operating and maintain full functionality and availability of the secure internet portal and 
related systems for online signature collection. Excepts a temporary removal, closure, or other 
limitation of acces s for the purpose of updating the system. In the event of tempor ary closure, 
requires the SOS to communicate to candidates the system’s status  and the estimated length of 
system downtime. Laws 2022, Chapter 266 
HB 2839: candidate nominations; signa tures; redistricting  (Bowers) Outlines the  signature 
requirements for the 2022 election cycle.  Specifies that, for supervisorial districts, justice precincts, 
and election precincts where new boundaries are adopted after January 2nd in a general election year 
and before nomination petitions are due, the required number of nomination petition signatures  is 
the number of qua lified signers in the new district.   An Emergency Measure that became ef fective 
on March 3, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 3 
SB 1008: elections; recount margin  (Ugenti-Rita) Modifies the criteria that triggers an auto matic 
election recount, when the margin between the two candidates receiving the greatest number of 
votes for a particular office, or between the number of votes cast f or and against a  measure or 
proposition, is less than or equal to 0.5 percent of the number of votes cast. Laws 2022, Chapter 230

<!-- page 301 -->
P a g e  | 19   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
ELECTIONS 
SB 1013: secretary of state; federal form (Townsend) Requires the Secretary of State to submit a 
request to the United States Election Assistance Commission that the Arizona federal voter 
registration form include the requirement to provide proof of citizenship , by December 31, 2022.   
Laws 2022, Chapter 268 
SB 1089: liens; fees; exemption (Shope) Prohibits county recorders from receiving a fee for a ny act 
necessary to record or release a restitution lien or any lien nece ssary to enforce a support order. 
Clarifies that a recording fee is not required to release a restitution lien.  Laws 2022, Chapter 9 
SB 1255: lieutenant governor; duties; ballot  (Hoffman) Specifies that n o later than 60 days before 
the date of the general election, a candidate for Governor is required to subm it to the Secretary of 
State the name of a person who will be the joint c andidate for Lieutenant Governor with that 
gubernatorial candidate and whose name will appear on the general election ballot jointly with that 
candidate. The Governor is required to appoint the Lieutenant Governor to serve as the Director of 
the Arizona Department of Administration (ADOA) or to fill any position for which the Gover nor is 
otherwise authorized by law to make an appointment. Conditionally enacted on the state 
Constitution being amended by the voters at the 2020 general election by passage of a n SCR 1024 
relating to the establishment of the office of Lieutenant Governor. Contains a delayed effective date 
for the term of office to begin in 2027. Laws 2022, Chapter 349 
SB 1260: registrations; early voting; move notice (Mesnard) Requires a county recorder to cancel an 
individual’s registration when the recorder receives confirmation from another county recorder that 
the individual has registered to vote in the other county, or the recorder receives and subsequently 
confirms that an individual has registered in a different county.  
Permits county recorders to remove persons confirmed to have registered in a different county from 
the active early voter list (AEVL) .  Requires individuals who receive the wrong early ballot to write 
“not at this address ” on the envelope and place it in a mailbox and directs the county recorder to 
proceed as if the United States Postal Service had returned an undeliverable ballot.  Makes it a Class 
5 felony to knowingly provide a mechanism for voting to another person who is registered in another 
state, including forwarding an early ballot addressed to the other person. Laws 2022, Chapter 270 
SB 1329: NOW: elections; counties; tabulation; posting (Boyer) Requires county recorders or other 
officers in charge of ele ctions to count the number of early ballots returned at voting locations on 
election day and post those totals on their website alongside the night’s last unofficial results , if 
practicable. Specifies that early ballots returned on election day must be entered into the county’s 
ballot tracking system beginning the day following the election. Laws 2022, Chapter 232 
SB 1355: campaign financ e report; due date  (Livingston) Changes the statutory due date for 
quarterly campaign finance reports to the third Monday in the month instead of the fifteenth day 
after the calendar quarter. Laws 2022, Chapter 196 
SB 1362: early ballot on -site tabulation (Mesnard) Permits the county recorder or other officer in 
charge of elections to provide on-site tabulation of qualified electors’ early ballots designated polling 
locations or voting centers on election day. Outlines the identification that qualified electors must 
present to have their early ballot tabulated on-site. Establishes procedures for having an early ballot 
tabulated on -site i ncluding: a) designating a physi cally separate area for processing; b) providing 
adequate poll workers, officials, and equipment; c) separately tallying and reporting the number of 
early ballots tabulated on-site; and d) reconciling the number of electors on the signature roster or 
e-pollbook with the number of completed early ballot affidavits and early ballots tabulated on -site. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 271

<!-- page 302 -->
P a g e  | 20   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
ELECTIONS 
SB 1460: election law amendments (Shope) Makes v arious changes relating to election law. 
Establishes regulations for adjustment of precinct boundaries based on r edistricting. Specifies if a 
voter surrenders an early ballot to the precin ct inspector, presents id entification as required by 
statute, and is not otherwise required to be issued a provisional ballot, the voter must be issued a 
standard ballot, and the pr ecinct inspector is required to retain the surrendered early ballot, 
unopened in its affidavit envel ope. Allows the notice of election to be posted online and at other 
locations where a government body regularly posts public notices if  there is not a newsp aper of 
general circulation in the election district. Modifies various dead lines. Requires the Secre tary of 
State to notify the appropriate county recorder if an existing voter becomes an A ddress 
Confidentiality Program participant and provide a substitute address.  Adds public official to the lists 
of persons that may request confidentiality with the residential address and telephone number 
contained in the voter registration application. Laws 2022, Chapter 277 
SB 1411: early ballots; tracking system  (Hoffman) Establishes a requirement on the creation of an 
early ballot tracking system to indicate if the voter's early ballot has been received and whether the 
early ballot has been verified and sent to be tabulated or rejected. Appropriates $250,000 from the 
state general fund in FY 2023  to the Secretary of State for enhancements to the Arizona Voter 
Information Database to provide for early ballot tracking . Contains a delayed effective date of 
January 1, 2024. Laws 2022, Chapter 358 
SB 1477: voter registration; felonies; clerk; database (Townsend) Requires the clerk of the Superior 
court to transmit a record containing the county’s felony convictions from the prior month to the 
Secretary of State (SOS) without charge. Specifies the record must contain th e name of the person 
convicted, their birth date, their SSN, parents’ names , if available, and their usual legal residence. 
Directs the SOS to use these records to facilitate the cancellation of voter registrations by 
forwarding them to the appropriate county recorder. Requires the county recorder to cancel the 
voter registration of the convicted felons included in the record. Laws 2022, Chapter 251 
SB 1638: early ballots; visually impa ired voters (Pace) Specifies that early voting includes voting 
using an accessib le vote -by-mail option for those who are blind or visually impaired. Directs the 
Secretary of State, upon completion of voting system certification, to require voting systems provide 
an accessible vote by the United States Postal Service  for persons who are blind or have a visual 
impairment. Establishes a delayed effective  date of January 1, 2023 , for Laws 2022, Chapt er 99 .  
Contains a delayed effective date of January 1, 2023, for section 1.  Laws 2022, Chapter 174

ENVIRONMENT AND WATER 
ENVIRONMENT AND WATER 
HB 2037: NOW: counties; power s; water supply projects  (Cook) Amends statute outlining the 
powers of county boards of supervisors to include entering into agreements with federal, state, and 
local governments for the acce ptance, management, and distribution of federal funds related to 
projects to increase water supply and availability. Specifies these powers must be used to the benefit 
of municipal water providers, irrigation districts used for agricultural purposes, or county flood 
control districts for the purposes of aquifer recharge. Laws 2022, Chapter 164 
HB 2054: water quality appeals board; continuation (Griffin) Extends the statutory life of the Water 
Quality Appeals Board by eight years, amending its termination date to July 1, 2030. Retroactively 
effective as of July 1, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 121 
HB 2057: water supply development fund; revisions (Griffin) Expands uses for monies in the Water 
Supply Development Revolving Fund to include providing techni cal assistance to water provider s 
for water supply development projects. Increases the  maximum amount of a single Water 
Infrastructure Finance Authority grant to $250,000. Laws 2022, Chapter 63 
HB 2101: electric energy; reliability; public policy  (Griffin) Makes a variety of changes to utility 
regulation, including requiring public power entities to establish an ombudsman office to investigate 
complaints and protect consumers .  R epeals requirements for  service territories to promote 
competition for electric generation service for all retail electric customers. Laws 2022, Chapter 191 
HB 2181: NOW: game and fish; taking wolves  (Cook) Establishes that the  Game and Fish 
Commission may not prohibit a person from taking a wolf that is actively threatening or attacking a 
person, livestock, or other domestic animal. Laws 2022, Chapter 223 
HB 2231: universities; water rights adjudication  (Griffin) Allows, to the extent permitted by court 
rule, a university under the jurisdiction of the Arizona Board of Regents to offer pro bono assistance 
to claimants who are small landowners and who lack counsel in the general stream adjudication of 
water rights. Specifies that universities providing such assistance may represent the cla imant until 
an evidentiary hearing or to some other contested proceeding. Requires all participating universities 
to cooperate and coordinate with the faculty of a coo perative extension that provides programs 
specified in statute. Requires the universities  to submit a written report of assistance activities to 
the Governor and the Legislature by November 15 each year.   Laws 2022, Chapter 152 
HB 2398: NOW: dude ranches; heritage trail p rogram (Dunn) Creates the Arizona Dude Ranch 
Heritage Trail Program  under the Arizona State Parks Board  (ASPB) to provide a guide for where 
there are working dude ranches in Arizona. Specifies criteria for successful applicants. Directs the 
ASPB to develop indicia and signage for program participants and to respond to applications in a 
timely manner. Specifies that the participants, taken together, are part of the Arizona Dude Ranch 
Heritage Trail. Laws 2022, Chapter 203 
HB 2406: water quality fee fund  (Griffin) Broadens the allowable uses of the Water Quality Fee 
Fund.  Makes several c hanges to the Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ) 
responsibilities and provides ADEQ more latitude in rulemaking for the Aquifer Protection Permit 
Program and Arizona Pollutant Discharge Elimination System. Laws 2022, Chapter 204 
HB 2409: multi -county water districts; storage tax  (Griffin) Extends the sunset date for a multi-
county water district’s ability to levy a tax no greater than $0.04 per $100 of assessed valuation , to 
December 31, 2029 .  Delays the reduction from a $0.04 cap to a $0.03 cap until January 1, 2030.  
Contains a delayed repeal date of January 2, 2035. Laws 2022, Chapter 154

<!-- page 304 -->
P a g e  | 22   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
ENVIRONMENT AND WATER 
HB 2410: environmental programs; amendments  (Griffin) Makes v arious changes to statutes 
regulating environmental programs. Repeals the article of statute regulating dry wells, including dry 
well registration and license to drill. Repeals the dust -free developments program. The Arizona 
Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ) is allowed, instead of required, to adopt rules for a ir 
pollution emission standards for off-road vehicles. Repeals session law requiring ADEQ to establish 
a daily visibility index to be used in evaluating and reportin g current visibility condi tions and 
progress toward visibility improvement goals. Establish es that an underground injection is 
prohibited if the injection is into a well that is subject to a drinking water protected use classification. 
The requirement for a property owner that has elected to leave contamination on the property that 
exceeds the a pplicable residential standard for the property to record with the county recorder a 
restrictive covenant for environmental use restriction, and applies only at a sit e remediated under 
programs, settlements, or orders administered by ADEQ. The deadline for  the U nited States  
Environmental Protection Agency to approve proposed modifications to the gasoline fuel 
formulation requirements as part of the State Implementation  Plan for air quality is e xtended two 
years, to July 1, 2024. Laws 2022, Chapter 177 
HB 2556: water infrastructure finance; sunset repeal  (Griffin) Repeals the termination date of the 
Water Infrastructure Finance Authority retroactive to July 1, 2022.   Laws 2022, Chapter 68 
HB 2580: wildfire management; de legation of au thority (Cook) Expands the State Forester’s 
responsibilities to include wildfire prevention, mitigation, and suppression activities delegated to 
Arizona by the United States. Requires the State Forester to obtain and maintain copies of initial and 
revised delegation of authority agreements. Laws 2022, Chapter 129 
SB 1067: NOW: cities; water infrastructure fina nce authority  (Shope) Prohibits the Water 
Infrastructure Finance Authority (WIFA) from providing funds to municipalities for the purposes of 
a) condemning, or acquiring through eminent  domain, assets of a public service corporation 
regulated by the Corporation Commission (unless the corporation certifies that it is a willing seller), 
and b) refinancing loans, bonds, or financing arrangements for the purposes of condemning or 
acquiring t he aforementioned assets. From FY 2023 through FY 2027, allows municipalities, 
counties, domestic water and wastewater improvement districts, and certain  sanitary districts to 
enter into financial assistance loan repayment agreements with WIFA without subm itting the 
question to the ballot. Laws 2022, Chapter 133 
SB 1146: fire suppression; assistance; private landowners (Kerr) Modifies how private landowners 
can qualify for financial assistance for fire -related infrastructure damage from the Arizona 
Department of Forestry and Fire Management (ADFFM) in FY 2021. Removes the requirement that 
the funds from ADFFM be the pay or of last resort and allows distributions to homeowners who 
disclose they do not qualify for other reimbursement. Requires ADFFM to prioritize distributions of 
financial assistance to private landowners. Laws 2022, Chapter 1 
SB 1147: NOW: pest management; certified applicator; fingerprinting (Kerr) Permits applicants for 
certification as “new” or “new qualified” pesticide applicators to meet the fingerprint require ment 
by submitting a current, u nexpired fingerprint clearance card. Adds session law that requires 
approval or denial of an application for certification as a new applicator within 120 d ays of the 
application’s receipt. Contains a delayed repeal date of July 1, 2023. An emergency measur e that 
became effective on March 23, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 32

<!-- page 305 -->
P a g e  | 23   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
ENVIRONMENT AND WATER 
SB 1197: NOW: WIFA projects; irrigation districts  (Shope) Permits Irrigation and Water 
Conservation Districts to utilize financial assistance provided by the Water Infrastructure Finance 
Authority (WIFA) to design or rehabilitate water infrastructure. Adds wastewater infrastructure 
and non point source project s to  the list of projects that qualify for WIFA financial assistance.  
Amends the definition of service area of an irrigation district as it pertains to an irrigation district 
that is not engaged in the withdrawal, delivery and distribution of groundwater as of the date of the 
designation of an Active Management Area to: a) include any areas of land that contain an operating 
system of canals, flumes, ditches and other works, rather than only areas as of the date of the AMA 
designation within the boundaries of the irrigation district;  b) require the operating system to be 
owned or operated by the irrigation district; and c) prohibit additional areas containing an operating 
system of canals, flumes, ditches and other works owned or operated  by the irrigation district from 
being added after December 31, 2027. Laws 2022, Chapter 213 
SB 1396: hydrogen study committee (Shope) Establishes a 12-member Hydrogen Study Committee 
(Committee) to evaluate existing laws and  make recommendations on legislation related to the 
production, distribution, and storage of hydrogen. The Committee is required to submit a report of 
its findings and reco mmendations to the Governor and the Legislature by September 1, 2023 .  An 
Emergency Measure that became effective on April 22, 2022 . Contains a de layed repeal date of 
October 1, 2024. Laws 2022, Chapter 173 
SB 1740: water infrastructure financing; supply; augmentation  (Kerr) Expands the powers and 
duties of the Water Infrastructure Finance Authority (WIFA) and transfe rs the authority, powers, 
duties, and responsibilities of specified water programs from the Arizona Finance Authority to 
WIFA. Authorizes WIFA to procure services  for the development, acquisition, construction, 
improvement, or equipping of water-related facilities using a variety of project delivery methods and 
forms of agreement; issue negotiable long-term water augmentation bonds; and to enter into public-
private partnership agreements that are certified by the Attorney General. Makes changes to 
eligibility requirements and processes for receiving financial assistance from the funds overseen by 
WIFA. Restricts WIFA from the purchase of mainstream Colorado River water rights. 
Establishes an 18-member WIFA Board to evaluate and approve funding requests for m onies from 
the Clean Water Revolving Fund, the Water Supply Development Revolving Fund, and the Long -
Term Water Augmentation Fund (LTWA Fund).  Establishes an 11-member Federal Water Programs 
Committee to review applications for assistance from the Clean Water Revolving Fund. Establishes 
the Water Conservation Grant Fund to facilitate voluntary water conservation programs or projects 
that meet specified requirements.  Establishes evaluation criteria for projects from the LTWA Fund 
and eligibility requirements for financial assistance from the LTWA Fund.  
Effective the month following the general effective date the State Treasurer is required to distribute 
$334 million proportionally for each month remaining in FY 2023 from the portion of revenues that 
are not designated as the distribution base to the LTWA Fund. Contains a legislative intent clause 
that specifies these distributions do not imp act the portion of transaction privilege tax ( TPT) 
revenues counties and municipalities receive as state shared revenu e. Appropriates $333 million 
from the state general fund in each of FY  2024 and FY 2025 to the LTWA Fund. Appropriates $3.5 
million from the state general fund in FY 2023 to the Department of Water Resources for an annual 
water supply and demand assessment required by this legislation. Contains a severability clause and 
a delayed termination date for WIFA of July 1, 2017. Laws 2022, Chapter 366

GENERAL GOVERNMENT 
GENERAL GOVERNMENT 
HB 2017: STEM funding; appropriation (Kaiser) Appropriates $8.6 million from the general fund in 
FY 2023 to the Maricopa Community Colleges for state aid for science, technology, engineering and 
mathematics (STEM) and workforce programs. Effective July 1, 2023, the statutorily required 
amounts appropriated from the general fun d to each community college district for STEM and 
workforce programs is no longer subject to appropriation. Laws 2022, Chapter 367 
HB 2107: emergency powers; business closure; repeal (Biasiucci) Removes the ability of mayors and 
chairmen of county boards of supervisors to order the closure of businesses following a declaration 
of emergency. Laws 2022, Chapter 86 
HB 2130: recreational users; property  (Griffin) Makes a variety of changes to statute outlining a 
landowner’s liabil ity for actions taken by recreational or educational users of their property .  
Specifies a landowner (or easement holder, lessee, tenant manager, or occupant) is not liable for 
damages in any civil action involving the land’s unknown conditions.  Specifies installing or failing to 
install a warning or modification does not create liability if there is no other basis for liability .  
Specifies it does  not create a duty or care or b asis of liability for injury to persons or property or 
relieve educational/recreational users from any obligation to exercise care in the use of land.  Makes 
any person that uses the land of another for recreational or educational purposes with or without 
permission liable for any damage to the land, property, livestock, or crops that the person may cause. 
Maintains the liability of any governmental entity, nongovernment al organization, or person that 
funds maintenance, performs maintenance, or supports improvements on land open to the public for 
educational and recreational purposes. Laws 2022, Chapter 242 
HB 2146: data security breach; notification  (Bolick) Requires any party doing business in Arizona 
that maintains or licenses unencrypted or unredacted personal information and experiences a 
security incident that impacts more than 1,000 persons, to provide notification the Arizona 
Department of Homeland Security (ADHS) Director using a form of their design. Specifies that a lack 
of a common form does not justify lack of submission. Speci fies that, aside from notification of 
individuals who had their information breached, all communic ations to the ADHS are confid ential 
and exempt from disclosure. Laws 2022, Chapter 81 
HB 2274: appropriation; stipend; kinship foster care  (Weninger) Appropriates $24.2 million from 
the state general fund in FY 2023 to the Department of Child Safety to use for a stipend of $300 per 
month for each child placed with the kinship foster care parent. Laws 2022, Chapter 329 
HB 2391: video service providers; enforcement; jurisdiction  (Dunn) Clarifies that when a city or 
county government files a complaint against a video service provider, or vice versa, the  Office of 
Administrative Hearings has original jurisdiction.  Laws 2022, Chapter 293 
HB 2488: uyghurs; forced labor; co ntracts; pro hibition (Biasucci) Prohibits a public entity  from 
entering a contract with a company unless the company certifies it does not use the following:         a) 
the forced labor of ethnic Uyghurs in China; b) any goods or services produced by such forced labor; 
or c) any contractors, subcontractors, or suppliers who use such labor or goods. Requires companies 
who provide these certifications and subsequently become aware of noncompli ant activities to 
notify the entity within five days, further specifying that companies who do not rectify their 
noncompliance within  180 days of such notificatio n will have th e contract terminated. Exempts 
contracts entered into before its effective date. Laws 2022, Chapter 295

<!-- page 307 -->
P a g e  | 25   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
GENERAL GOVERNMENT  
HB 2587: public records; point of contact  (Grantham) Requires entities subject to publi c records 
requests to provide the name and contact information of an employee or department authorized and 
able to provide the information requested. Specifies if no employee or department is authorized to 
provide the inf ormation requested, the entity is required to provide contact information for those 
able to forward the request to an authorized party. Requires publication of the aforementioned 
contact information on the website maintained by the entity. Specifies that, for entities unable to 
maintain an online records request portal that provides receipts, the individual receiving the request 
must acknowledge the request’s receipt within five business days. Laws 2022, Chapter 142 
HB 2 610: NOW: affordable housing; project unit size  (Burges) Property used exclusively for 
affordable r ental housing is no longer limited to 200 units or less to qualify for a property tax 
exemption. Laws 2022, Chapter 287 
HB 2626: animal handling; microchip scan  (Kavanagh) A county pound, municipal facility, a 
veterinarian, animal  shelter, or an Arizona incorporated humane society with a cooperative 
agreement with a county are required to thoroughly scan all dogs and cats for the presence of a 
microchip and make a reasonable effort t o contact the owner. These facilities are also re quired to 
scan all deceased dogs and cats found in a public place and brought to the facility for the presence of 
a microchip and make a reasonable effort to contact the owner. Laws 2022, Chapter 207 
HB 2678: state broadband office  (Cobb) Establishes the State Broadband Office in the Arizona 
Commerce Authority and establishes responsibilities of the o ffice staff. Th is o ffice is required to 
publish and maintain a state broadband plan. Laws 2022, Chapter 384 
HB 2679: employment discrimination; sex ual harassment; defi nition (Wilmeth)  Expands the 
definition of employer to any person, or an agent of any person, who is alleged to have discriminated 
against anyone for opposing  sexual harassment or participating in resulting proceedings .  Laws 
2022, Chapter 169 
HB 2741: state licensing; fee waiver  (Osborne) Expands the list of persons that state agencies are 
required to waive any initial license fee charged for, with some exceptions, to include any active-duty 
military service member's spouse, and any honorably discharged veteran who has been discharged 
no more than two  years before application. Appropriates $300,000 from the state general fund in 
each fiscal year to the Department of Administration to reimburse agencies on a first -come, first-
served basis for fees the agency waived. Laws 2022, Chapter 336 
SB 1001: NOW: extortion; social media messages (Leach) Amends the statutory definition of theft 
by extortion to incl ude exposing a secret or an asserted fact in a social media message. Exempts 
situations where the threat is based on a plausible claim of right to the property or services obtained.  
Laws 2022, Chapter 254 
SB 1021: health care liens; limitation (Ugenti-Rita) Prescribes restrictions to liens made by a health 
care provider or an ambulance entity from recovering an injured person’s medical paym ent from a 
third-party judgement, settlement or award .  Requires all  interested parties, including the health  
care provider, patient, and patient's attorney, to compromise on any lien or assignment granted and 
the amounts owed to provide a settlement of the claim that is fair and equitable to all parties. Factors 
that must be considered in determining the extent of the compromise are listed.  Applies to liens that 
are filed for services provided beginning January 1, 2023. Laws 2022, Chapter 340

<!-- page 308 -->
P a g e  | 26   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
GENERAL GOVERNMENT  
SB 1174: appropriation; center street; Jerome (Gowan) Modifies a $560,000 FY 2021 appropriation 
to reflect the correct street in Jerome. Laws 2022, Chapter 33 
SB 1179: NOW: video service; boundary change; definition (Gowan) For the purpose of county and 
municipal video service provider regulations, modifies the definition of “video service” t o exclude 
streaming content and direct -to-home satellite services. Modifies the definition of "video service 
provider" to exclude video programing offered via the internet and may include streaming content. 
Specifies that a video service provider cannot be  required to pay license fees on gross revenues 
within the area encompassed by a county or municipal boundary change for at least 30 days a fter 
the notification of a boundary change is provided.  Laws 2022, Chapter 212 
SB 1278: NOW: labor benefits organization; damages; definitions (Mesnard) An employee who has 
suffered calculated damages is authorized to recove r three times the calculated damages from a 
"third-party benefit s organization" (defined) for the cost of benefits paid by the employee. An 
employer that has suffered calculated damages is authorized to recover three times the calculated 
damages from a thi rd-party benefits organization that paid for the cost of employe e benefits. 
Establishes a formula for calculated damages and specifies circumstances under which an employee 
is allowed to recover calculated damages attributable to monies paid by the employe e's employer. 
Establishes circumstances under which a third-party benefits organization is not liable under these 
provisions. A claim for ca lculated damages may be brought against a third -party benefits 
organization in superior court or any other appropria te venue. Applies to any third -party benefits 
organization that establishes or negotiates the rates or collects monies for employee benefits  on 
behalf of an Arizona resident or an employer that has employees in Arizona. Some exceptions. 
Severability clause.  Laws 2022, Chapter 255 
SB 1710: sports; tourism; film authority; app ropriation (Gowan) Establishes the Southern Ariz ona 
Sports, Tourism and Film Authority (Authority) as a corporate and political body consisting of the 
counties of Greenlee, Graham, Cochise, Santa Cruz, Pima, and Pinal. The Authority is governed by a 
13-member board, and powers and duties of the board are established. Outlines the requirements 
for the Authority to issue revenue bonds. Appropriates $500,000 from the state general fund in  FY 
2023 to the Office of Tourism for funding the Authority. Laws 2022, Chapter 364 
SB 1712: criminal victim notification fund; appropriation (Gowan) Establishes the Law Enforcement 
Crime Victim Notification Fund (Fund), to be administered by the State Treasurer and used to fund 
software that enables the county -based deployment of an automated crime victim notification 
system to a user base consisting of law enforcement agencies. Appropriates $3.8 million from the 
state general fund in FY 2 023 to the Fund and appropriates $1.4 million to the FY 2023  to the 
Victims' Rights Fund. Laws 2022, Chapter 333 
SB 1721: appointment; ombudsman -citizens aide  (Barto) Appoints Joanne MacDonnell as the  
Ombudsman-Citizens Aide for a term ending June 30, 2027.  An Emergency Measure that became 
effective on July 6, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 365

HEALTH AND HUMAN SERVICES 
HEALTH AND HUMAN SERVICES 
HB 2083: AHCCCS services; diabetes management  (Osborne) Expands t he list of medically 
necessary health and medical serv ices the Arizona Health Care Cost Containment System 
(AHCCCS) may provide to include up to 10 program hours of diabetes outpatient self-management 
training services annually, if prescribed by a primary care practitioner. Specifies monies from the 
Hospital Assessment Fund cannot be used to provide diabetes outpatient self-management training 
services. Laws 2022, Chapter 328 
HB 2086: DHS; school immunizations; exclusions (Osborne) Specifies that an immunization against 
COVID-19 or any variant of COVID -19 is not req uired for school attendance. Requires all 
immunization to be prescribed by a rule adopted by the Department of Health Se rvices before the 
immunization may be required for in-person school attendance. Laws 2022, Chapter 240 
HB 2120: NOW: workers' compensation; injury reports  (Kaiser) Specifies for the purpose of 
workers' compensation injury reports, employers are only requi red to report injuries requiring 
medical treatment, which does not include any one-time, short-term treatment by nonmedical staff 
that requires little technology  or training to administer. For any workers' compensation claim 
involving "medical -only loss" (d efined), any experience rating adjustment as determined by a 
national nonprofit insurance rating organization must be applied to reduce the impact of loss in th e 
employer's experience modification calculation. Laws 2022, Chapter 368 
HB 2 157: supplemental appropriations; community -based services  (Cobb) Makes supplemental   
FY 2022 appropriations to align state spending w ith American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) resources .  
Appropriates $30.1 million from the Children’s Insurance Program Fund and $1.1 billion to the 
Arizona Health Care Cost Containment System to implement ARPA’s home and community-based 
services spending plan  and adjust funding formula requirements . Provides $362.7 million to the 
Department of Economic Security’s (DES)  Division of Developmental Disabilities to implement 
ARPA’s home and community -based services spending plan.  Makes a supplemental FY 2022 
appropriation of $15.1 million to the Department of Child Safety to continue existing functionality, 
oversight, and to claim federa l monies. Establishes reporting requirements relating to these 
appropriations. Laws 2022, Chapter 2 
HB 2336: licensed mental health professional; definition (Payne) Expands the definition of "licensed 
mental health professionals" that may provide traumatic event counseling for public safety 
employees, to include psychiatrists, mental health nurse practitioners, psychiatr ic clinical nurse 
specialists, and physician assistants. Contains a delayed repeal date of  January 1, 2027, to  repeal 
traumatic event counseling for public safety employees. Laws 2022, Chapter 377 
HB 2371: NOW: COVID vaccine mandates; prohibition (Bolick) Prohibits any governmental entity 
from requiring a person under 18 to receive a COVID-19 vaccination without their parent or 
guardian’s consent. States that a person who violates this section is guilty of a Class 1 misdemeanor. 
Authorizes the county attorney to prosecute related violations.  Laws 2022, Chapter 263 
HB 2374: acute care services; pilot program  (Cobb) Allows the health care services provided to 
patients in the Acute Care Services at Home Pilot Program (Program), to include services of mobile 
paramedics, at the option of the hospital . Establishes requirements for mobile paramedi cs 
participating in the Program,  including at least two years of experience as a paramedic and being 
certified in community paramedicine by a recognized certifying body be fore January 1, 2023. 
Nursing or other health care services provided under the Program, other than the twice-daily onsite 
patient assessment visits, may be virtual or in person.  Laws 2022, Chapter 140

HEALTH AND HUMAN SERVICES 
HB 2397: emotional abuse; vulnerable adults  (Dunn) Expands the def inition of abuse for Adult 
Protective Services statutes to include emotional abuse. Laws 2022, Chapter 379 
HB 2407: ambulances; mileage rate ca lculation (Griffin) Requires the  Department of Health 
Services to incorporate the entire list of specified factors when calculating the proposed mileage 
rate for ambulance services, including the cost o f fuel, vehicle maintenance and insurance, and the 
cost of licensure and registration of each ground ambulance vehicle.  Laws 2022, Chapter 217 
HB 2453: governmental entities; mask requirement; prohibition (Carter) Prohibits a governmental 
entity from  imposing any requirement to wear a mask or face covering anywhere on the 
governmental entity's premises, except where long-standing workplace safety and infection control 
measures that are unrel ated to COVID -19 may be required . Exempts special health care districts 
from this prohibition.  Laws 2022, Chapter 247 
HB 2498: covid-19; vaccination requirements; prohibition (Hoffman) Prohibits a government entity 
from requiring an Arizona resident to receive a vaccination for COVID -19 or any of i ts variants. 
Exempts health care institutions owned or operated by governmental entities from this prohibition. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 180 
HB 2601: kratom products; definitions  (Kavanagh) For the purpose of statutes regulating kratom 
products, the terms "processor" and "retai ler" (both defined) replace the term "dealer." Violations 
and enforcement of kratom product regulations are also governe d by statutes governing food 
control.  Laws 2022, Chapter 253 
HB 26 16: mask mandates; minors; parental consent  (Chaplik) Prohibits the state, its political 
subdivisions, other governmental entities, and any school district or charter school from requiring 
person under 18 years of age wear a mask or face covering without their parent or guardian’s express 
consent.  Laws 2022, Chapter 184 
HB 2622: AHCCCS; eligibility  (Blackman) Requires the Arizona Health Care Cost Containment 
System Administration  (AHCCCS) to annuall y renew on an automatic basis the eligibility of an 
individual who was in the custody of the Department of Child Safety when the individual reached 18 
years of age without requiring additional information from the individual until the individual reaches 
26 years of age, unless the individual notifies the AHCCCS Administration that the individual moved 
out of Arizona or has provided information in dicating that the individual may qualify for a different 
eligibility category .  Conditionally enacted subject to the approval of the federal Centers for 
Medicare and Medicaid Services.  Laws 2022, Chapter 339 
HB 2691: health care workforce; grant programs (Osborne) Establishes health care workforce grant 
programs within Arizona Health Care Cost Containment System (AHCCCS) Administration and the 
Department of Health Services (DHS).  Appropriates state general fund monies in each of FY 2023 
through FY 2027 for the following: a)  $15 million to the DHS for the Arizona Nurse Education 
Investment Program, to increase the capacity of nursing education programs in Arizona;                        b) 
$27 million from the DHS for the Nurse Clinical Rotation and Licensed Nurse Tr aining Program, to 
expand the capacity of preceptor training programs at health care institutions for nursing students 
and licensed nurses ; and c) $500,000 to DHS for the Precep tor Grant Program, to expand the 
capacity of preceptor training programs for gr aduate students pursuing degrees as physicians or 
advanced practice registered nurses.  Appropriates $5 million from the state general fund in each of 
FY 2023 through FY 2025 to AHCCCS for deposit in the Demonstration Project Program Fund to 
expand the cap acity of the Maricopa County Community College District and the Navajo County 
Community College District to train students as behavioral health workers. Laws 2022, Chapter 330

HEALTH AND HUMAN SERVICES 
SB 1009: state of emergency; executive powers  (Ugenti-Rita) Repeals reforms to gubernatorial 
powers that were struck down by the Arizona Supreme Court due to their unconstitutional inclusion 
in the FY 2022 budget (Arizona School Boards Association et al  v. State of Arizona). Beginning 
January 2, 2023, i mposes new r estrictions on the Governor’s powers to proclaim a state of 
emergency (SOE) for a public health emergency (PHE), including:  a) l imiting the duration of a 
Governor’s initial SOE for a PHE to 30 days; b) p ermitting the Governor to extend the initial 
declaration in 30-day increments until the PHE reaches a maximum length of 120 days; c) requiring 
extensions of the PHE past  120 days to receive a supportive concurrent resolution from the 
Legislature; d) specifies the Legislature may further e xtend th e PHE without limit, restricts any 
extensions to 30-day increments ; and e) p rohibits the Governor, if their PHE does not rec eive 
support from the Legislature, from proclaiming a new state of emergency based on the same 
conditions. 
Requires new reporting to the Legislature from the Executive in the event of a PHE, including:           a) 
requiring the Governor to submit a written report to the Legislature alongside any extension of the 
SOE for a PHE ; b) a fter the SOE for a PHE has reached 60 days, requi res t he director of the 
Department of Health Services (DHS) and a representative of the Governor to brief the Legislature 
on a potential  extension; and c) r equires the Legislature’s recommendation on extension to be 
conspicuously posted on the Governor’s and the DHS websites. Laws 2022, Chapter 220 
SB 1346: state employees; vaccination inquiries prohibited  (Rogers) Prohibits the state, any state 
agency, or any contractor for Arizona from sending its employees to inquire about a person's 
vaccination status on a door-to-door basis. Laws 2022, Chapter 354 
SB 1 494: covid-19 vaccine; unemployment insurance  (Mesnard) Prohibits t he D epartment of 
Economic Security from disqualifying an individual from receiving unemployment insurance benefits 
on the basis o f the individual's separation from employment if the individual was terminated from 
employment for not receiving a COVID -19 vaccine  or COVID -19 booster s hot required by the 
employer. Unemployment insurance benefits paid to an individual in this circumstanc e cannot be 
charged against an employer's account if the employer's requirement that employees receive the 
COVID-19 vaccine or COVID-19 booster shot is required by law. Laws 2022, Chapter 360 
SB 1651: serious mental illness; annual report  (Barto) Requires the Arizona Health Care Cost 
Containment System (AHCCCS) Administration to annually report to the Joint Legislative Budget 
Committee and the chairpersons of the Health and Human Services Committees of the Senate and 
the House of Representatives on a list of sp ecified information relating to individuals living with 
serious mental illness.  Requires the AHCCCS Administration to develop  a methodology to track 
accommodations that are requested and provided pursuant to the Americans with Disabilities Act 
to persons with disabilities who are living with serious mental illness, and to report on the 
methodology to the Legislature by December 31, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 305

PLANNING, ZONING, AND LAND USE 
HB 2126: hazardous waste program fees (Griffin) Allows the Arizona Department of Environmental 
Quality (ADEQ) to increase  fees for its hazardous waste program regarding per-ton fees for 
generation and disposal of the waste.  Removes the requirement for ADEQ to receive public notice 
and comment period and subsequent review by the Joint Legislative Budget Committee prior to the 
fee increase. Similarly repeals the post -September 30, 2013, prohibition on these fees increasing 
without specific statutory authority. Laws 2022, Chapter 87  
HB 2255: fireworks; permissible use; diwali  (Biasiucci) Allows the use of permissible consumer 
fireworks is expanded to include two days before t he first day of Diwali through the third day of 
Diwali, and the d ates that those counties and municipalities cannot prohibit the us e of permissible 
consumer fireworks is expanded to include the second and third days of Diwali in counties with a 
population of more than 500,000 persons. Laws 2022, Chapter 372 
HB 2411: coal combustion residuals program  (Griffin) Requires the director of  the Arizona 
Department of En vironmental Quality ( ADEQ) to adopt rules to establish and operate a coal 
combustion residuals (CCR) program equivalent to or at least as protective as the f ederal CCR 
program for the purpose of obtaining approval to operate the federal CCR program. Requires these 
rules to include requirements for issuing, denying, suspending or modifying individual CCR permits. 
States that federal CCR regulations may be adopted by reference. Laws 2022, Chapter 178 
HB 2455: incorporation; urban areas  (Carter) Amends statute relating to incorporation  to require  
interested parties to provide written notice to the board of supervisors (BOS) of their intention to 
circulate a copy of the petition for incorporation at least six months before publishing a copy of the 
petition and publish the petition in a newspaper of general circulation in the area six months after 
submission of written notice to the BOS. Specifies that an area to be incorporated can include large 
areas of uninhabited, rural, or farmlands if the land has been previously platted for housing or 
commercial development by the BOS, or the current owner agrees to include the proper ty in the 
proposed incorporation area.  Amends the defin ition of “uninhabited” to include land owned by a 
mining or metallurgical company.   Specifies that planned community associat ions (PCA) under 
declarant control may notify the BOS of its request to be excluded from the petition to incorporate. 
Allows the BOS to  proceed with the incorporation of the proposed area without the PCA if 
population requirements are met. Laws 2022, Chapter 108  
HB 2579: residential zoning; park model trailers  (Cook) Requires counties to allow the use of park 
model trailers (PMT) as accessory dwelling units (ADU) for use as a single-family residence zoned for 
one dwelling unit per three acres or greater.   Specifies the PMT must be on a semi -permanent or 
permanent foundation, have a utility connection, and comply with draina ge, floodplain, sanitary 
sewer, or septic waste disposal require ments, along with other zoning standards. Permits a county 
to require a building permit and inspection for installation of a PMT. Prohibits usage on parcels 
located in a high noise or accident potential zone. Laws 2022, Chapter 182

<!-- page 313 -->

PLANNING, ZONING, AND LAND USE 
HB 2660: liquor; licensing; processes; procedures  (Weninger) Makes v arious changes to statutes 
relating to liquor licenses. Authorizes the owner or management of a regional shopping center that 
encompasses at least 400,000 square feet of retail space, on behalf of retail licensees located at the 
shopping center, to apply for an extension of premises to allow on-sale retail liquor licensees to sell 
spirituous liquor and to allow patrons to consume spirituous liquor in a designated pedestrian area 
of the regional shopping center. Specified, t he applicati on proces s, including review by the local 
governing body and submission of plans or diagrams designating the specific extension of premises 
requested. Establishes conditions for an extension of premises. Authorizes a n on -sale spirituous 
liquor licensee to apply to the Departmen t of Liquor Licenses and Control to extend the licensed 
premises on an individual day or hour basis or on a regular recurring basis. Authorizes the Governor 
to issue an executive order that extends the closing time of liquor licensees  until 3:00 a.m. for 
spirituous liquor sales in connection with a professional or collegiate national sporting championship 
event held in Arizona. Laws 2022, Chapter 282 
HB 2674: NOW: hou sing supply study committee (Kaiser) Establishes an 11 -member Housing 
Supply Study Committee (Committee) to review data on the scope of housing supply and access ; 
compile an overview of ways to address  Arizona’s housing  shortage and mitigate its causes ; and 
solicit ideas from indu stry representatives subject matter experts, and the community. Directs the 
Committee to submit a report of its findings and recommendations to the Governor and the 
Legislature by December 31, 2022.  Repeals the Committee on July 1, 2023. An Emergency Measure 
that became effective on April 25, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 185 
HB 2724: assisted living; distance requirements; prohibition  (Toma) Requires a county or 
municipality with a zoning ordinance that  restricts the distance between assisted living homes to 
establish an administrative procedure in which a deviation from the separation requirement may be 
granted. Requires the county or municipality to make newly registered assisted living homes, or 
those who have submitted a land use application for an assisted living home, aware of the 
administrative procedure to request a reasonable accommodation. Specifies zoning ordi nances 
must measure distance between assisted living homes based on a linear foot basis from lot line to lot 
line. Laws 2022, Chapter 132 
SB 1136: public works; contracts; payments (Gray) Inserts additional language in statute governing 
state procurement of construction and other specified services. Permits a contractor, when asked 
by an owner in writing to perform changed or additional work and after the contractor has submitted 
a reasonable cost estimate of the changed or additional work,  to request payment for changed or 
additional work completed during the preceding month in monthly pay estimates. Specifies that the 
person designa ted in the construction contract  will make an interim determination for payment 
approval purposes and certify for payment an amount they believe to be reasonably justified. States 
that either party may disagree with the interim determination and assert a claim. Contains additional 
language that provides the aforementioned monthly pay estimate opportunity to the subcontractors 
working under the contractors. Laws 2022, Chapter 31

PLANNING, ZONING, AND LAND USE 
SB 1168: vacation rentals; short -term rentals; enforcement  (Mesnard) Authorizes counties and 
municipalities to require short-term rentals (STR) owners to pro vide the following: a) a contact for 
24-hour response to complaints/emergencies; b) obtain a local regulatory permit; c) provide 
notification to adjacent residential prope rty owners; d) display the permit number on each 
advertisement for STR and transaction privilege tax (TPT) license; and e) maintain liability insurance 
unless provided through an online lodging marketplace.  Prescribes the authoriz ed penalties a 
county or munici pality may charge for verified violations and failure to obtain a local permit.   
Requires the Department of Revenue to suspend, for a period of 12 months the TPT license of the 
owner of a vacation rental or short-term rental that has three verified violations within the same 12-
month period, after a hearing. Laws 2022, Chapter 343 
SB 1238: state lands; appraisals; leases; rights-of-way (Kerr) Increases, the number of days that may 
pass between the Board of Appeals’ approval of lease, exchange, or sale of property from 180 to 240, 
without requiring the reappraisal or update of Arizona State Land Department’s original appraisal of 
the property.  Specifies that approval of a lease for commercial purposes from the Board of Appeals 
is required only if the lease is for a term longer than ten years. Laws 2022, Chapter 14 
SB 1257: long-term recreational vehicle parks; caregivers (Mesnard) Allows a resident recreational 
vehicle (RV) park with a disability to have a caregiver occupy the RV to provide live-in health care. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 16 
SB 1275: fireworks; use; overnight hours; prohibition (Mesnard) Allows counties and municipalities 
to prohibit the use of permissible consumer fireworks between the hours of 11:00 p.m. and 8:00 a.m. 
Excepts specified hours on New Year’s Eve and Independence day between 11:00 p.m. and 1:00 a.m. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 18 
SB 1376: codes; ordinanc es; use of refrigerants  (Gray) Prohibits municipalities and counties  from 
adopting any code, ordinance, or general plan provision that prohibits the use of refrigerants deemed 
acceptable by the Clean Air Act (CAA). Specifies that any equipment used must be listed and installed 
in compliance with Clean Air Act regulations. Laws 2022, Chapter 22 
SB 1594: state lands; annexation; delegation; minerals (Kerr) Permits municipalities and developers 
to enter into pre -annexation agreements with property owners agreeing to future annexation of 
their property. Exempts proper ty owners with pre -annexation agreements from inclusion in the 
annexation petition. Stat es that the property and property owner  will be included in the various 
calculations of real and personal property value for the purposes of accurately ascertaining val ue, 
regardless of the pre-annexation agreement. Laws 2022, Chapter 215

PUBLIC PERSONNEL AND RETIREMENT 
HB 2063: PSPRS; CORP; reemployment; time period (Blackman) Reduces the length of time needed 
before a retired me mber of the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) or the 
Corrections Officer Retirement Plan (CORP) can be reemployed by their former employer to             6-
months after their retirement, from 12 -months. Clarifies the exemption for members to retur n to 
work earlier than 6-months if they go through an open, competitive process does not apply  “if the 
retired member has a prearranged reemployment agreement with the employer.”  Laws 2022, Chapter 24 
SB 1002: NOW: EORP; appropriation; resignation; retirement (Leach) Allows an  elected official 
who is a member of the Elected Officials' Retirement Plan (EORP) and who  gives an irrevocable 
notice of resignation on or before July 29, 2022, with a date of resignation and retirement of no later 
than September 30, 2022, to receive pension payments for normal retirement if the member is either 
55 years of age with 10 years of credited service or is 59.5 years of age with five years of credited 
service. Prohibits a member who retires under these provisions and who subsequently becomes an 
elected official from making contributions to the EORP plan, accruing credited service under th e 
plan, and participating in the EORP defined contribution system. Appropriates $60 million from the 
state general fund in FY 2023  to the EORP fund for the EORP unfun ded accrued liability. An 
Emergency Measure that became effective on June 28, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 323 
SB 1081: NOW: PSPRS; advisory committee (Livingston) Removes the statutory requirement  that 
the Senate President and Ho use Speaker’s appointees to the Public Safety Personnel Retirement 
System Advisory Committee are either a legislative staffer or a legislator. Laws 2022, Chapter 72 
SB 1082: ASRS; lease-purchase agreements; contributions; prepayment  (Livingston) Allows a n 
Arizona State Retirement System (ASRS) employer to p repay the employer's required pension 
contributions directly to ASRS according to a written agreement between the employer and ASRS . 
Allows prepaid pension contributions to be deposited directly in the ASRS Trust Fund or a Section 
115 Trust. Specifies the earnings accrual rate is the ASRS total pension fund  rate of return. Allows 
prepaid contributions and accrued earnings to be  used solely to reduce the employer's fu ture 
pension contributions. An Emergency Measure that became effective on June 28, 2022 .                         
Laws 2022, Chapter 324 
SB 1083: ASRS; modifications (Livingston) Makes a variety of clarifying changes to statute related to 
the Arizona State Retirement System (ASRS) . Clarifies that employees eligible for PSPRS plans are 
not eligible to participate in a n ASRS supplemental employee deferra l plan. Adds a requirement to 
elect early retirement, specifying that the member must have terminated employment with all 
employers. Requires tha t only the principal amount paid for service credit purchases may be 
transferred to another defined benefit ret irement system if an employee can transfer to another 
system. Removes language related to interest distributions if a member dies prior to the start of the 
distribution if the member has  no designated beneficiary or if the deceased members surviving 
spouse dies after the member but before receiving distributions. Removes the permissive authority 
of ASRS to make payments on behalf of incapacitated members. Adds “a simple retirement account 
that satisfies the requirements of § 408(p) of the Internal Revenue Code” to the definition of “eligible 
retirement plan” for distributions made after December 18, 2015. Clarifies that overpayments must 
be repaid by the individual who received the payment.  Laws 2022, Chapter 145

PUBLIC PERSONNEL AND RETIREMENT 
SB 1084: public retirement systems; administration (Livingston) Makes clarifying changes to statute 
governing the state’s public retirement systems, including:  
• Requires employers participating in the Cancer Insurance Program to include the premium  
amount as wages subject to income and employment taxes and pay the premium to the System 
to the extent necessary to exclude coverage from the gross income of participants. 
• Adds Roth IRAs (after January 1, 2008), and simple retirement accounts (after December 18, 
2015) to the definition of “eligible retirement  plan” that can accept rollover distributions in 
Elected Officials’ Retirement Plan ( EORP) and Corrections Officer Retirement Plan (CORP) 
statute. 
• Adds s imple retirement accounts (after December 18, 2015) to the de finition of “eligible 
retirement plan” in statute relating to the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS). 
• Adds “game  rangers who are certified peace officers and who are employed by an Indian 
reservation” to the list of groups eligible for PSPRS. 
• Repeals language struck down by the Ariz ona Supreme Court due to its unconstitutional 
inclusion in the FY 2022 budget (Arizona School Boards Association et al v. State of Arizona). 
• Moves the deadline for the submission of various reports to the Governor f rom the Board of 
Trustees (concerning EORP, PSPRS, CORP, the Elected Officials’ Defined Contribution 
Retirement System [EODCRS], the Public Safety Personnel Defined Contribution Retirement 
Plan [PSPDCRP] and the Elected Officials’ Defined Contribution Retirement System Disability 
Program [EODCRSDP]) from December 31  of every year to December 1 . This provision was 
included in language previously struck down for unrelated reasons. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 73 
SB 108 5: PSPRS; funded ratio; asset transfers  (Livingston) Makes changes to the Public Safety 
Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) statutes to allow for the implementation of the revised 
actuarial funding policy recommended by the PSPRS Advisory Committee, including:  
• Eliminating the requirement that, post FY ‘07, “the employer contribution rate shall not be less 
than 8 percent of compensation.” 
• Raises, to 100 percent from 50 percent, the percentage of “present value of all future benefits 
of the employer” that must be kept in a stabilization reserve account by specified employers. 
• Permits employers with > 100 percent funded ratio to use excess contributions from specified 
employees (those paying above 7.65 percent) to reduce employer contributions. Specifies that 
employers with < 100 percent funded ratio “shall accumulate” contributions until their funded 
ratio exceeds 100 percent. 
• Amends conditions that a fund must meet for the board to suspend contributions, specifying 
that an actuary must determine that the employer’s stabilization reserve is funded to          100 
percent of the present value of all future benefits, and the board determines that suspending 
the contributions would not conflict with its fiduciary responsibility.  
• Permits employers to request “that the board transfer excess assets of an employer’s account 
that has no liabilities or beneficiaries to another account of the employer t hat is managed by 
the board” if the employer meets certain requirements. 
 Laws 2022, Chapter 221

SB 1086: appropriation; unfunded liability; PSPRS; CORP  (Livingston) Makes a supplement al 
appropriation from the general fund in FY  2022 of a total of $87. 7 million to the Public Safety 
Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) to be depo sited in specified PSPRS and Corrections Officer 
Retirement Plan (CORP) group employer accounts for specified state employees. The PSPRS board 
is required to account for these appropriations in the June 30, 2022, actuarial valuation of the PSPRS 
groups and  CORP groups listed, and account for these appropriations when calculating the 
employee contribution rates and employer contribution rates during FY 2024.                                       Laws 
2022, Chapter 325 
SB 1268: PSPRS; deferred retirement option plan  (Livingston) Allows, on agreement of the 
employer, for a Tier 1 Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) member meeting certain 
age and service to continue serving an additional 24 months in the DROP program. All fund s 
accumulated during the first 60 mon ths of the employee ’s DROP account ar e transferred into a 
defined contribution account for the remaining time in the program, up to 24 months. Additionally , 
requires PSPRS to report stakeholder recommendations to improve retention and recruitment of 
Tier 2 and 3 PSPRS members by December 31, 2022. Laws 2022, Chapter 351

TAXATION AND PUBLIC FINANCE 
TAXATION AND PUBLIC FINANCE 
HB 2156: NOW: tax credits; motion picture cre dits (Cobb) Establishes in tax year  (TY) 2023 an 
individual and corporate income tax credit against production costs paid by a motion picture 
production company subject to Arizona taxes that are direc tly attributable to a motion p icture 
production. Specifies credit is a pe rcentage of the qualified production costs as approved by the 
Arizona Commerce Authority  (Authority), based on the total amount spent by the production 
company. Establishes requiremen ts for a motion picture produc tion company to qualify for the 
credit. The maximum aggregate amount of tax credits is $75 million in calendar year (CY) 2023, $100 
million in CY 2024, and $125 million in CY 2025 and after. The Authority is r equired to implement 
an Arizona Motion Picture Production Program (Program) and certify motion picture production 
companies that produce one or more motion pictures in Arizona for the purpose of the tax credits. 
Establishes requirements for a production company to qualify for the Program. Contains a delayed 
repeal date of January 1, 2044. Laws 2022, Chapter 387 
HB 2320: veterans' organization; leased property; classification (Kavanagh) Classifies property and 
buildings leased to a veterans’ organization as Class 9  property for property tax assessment . 
Specifies the veterans’ organization must annually certify it is the sole beneficiary of the 
classification and that the property is used primarily for its operations. States that a veterans’ 
organization that files evidence of its tax-exempt status with the county assessor may be exempted 
from the requirement to  file annual affidavits. States  that a property owned by a vete rans’ 
organization and leased to another veterans’  organization is exempt from taxation.                                       
Laws 2022, Chapter 261 
HB 2629: property tax liens; expiration dates (Barton) Extends the deadline for a county treasurer 
to notify a purchaser of an expiring property tax lien to within a year of the expiration date. 
Additionally, extends the time line for post -expiration notification of the purchaser to 30 days 
instead of seven days of the expiration. Laws 2022, Chapter 69 
HB 2693: tax credit; charitable organizations; adjustme nt (Toma) Requires the  Department of 
Revenue to adjust the dollar amounts of the tax credit for contributions to qualifying chari table 
organizations according to the average annual change  in the metropolitan Phoenix consumer price 
index beginning in tax year 2023. Specifies the dollar amounts cannot be revised below the amounts 
for the prior tax year. Laws 2022, Chapter 385 
HB 2699: fire district bonds; merger; consolidation  (Martinez) Specifies, in instances where  a fire 
district with outstanding bonded  indebtedness is merged or consolidated  with other fire di stricts, 
that the indebtedness will not be assumed by all of the new district and shall instead be considered 
indebtedness of the district that originally approved the bonds. States that the bonds will be paid by 
an annual tax levied by the board of supervisors (BOS) on the prior district’s taxable property  and 
not on the entire newly merged or consolidated district.  Laws 2022, Chapter 84 
HB 2822: personal property; additional deprecia tion (Weninger) For personal property that is 
initially classified during or after tax year 2022 as Class 1, Class 2 (P), or Class 6, the county assessor 
is required to use a valuation factor of 2.5 percent. Previously, the valuation ranged from 25 percent 
of the scheduled depreciated value in the first tax year of assessment to 89 percent of the scheduled 
depreciated value in the fifth tax year of assessment. Laws 2022, Chapter 103

<!-- page 319 -->
P a g e  | 37   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
TAXATION AND PUBLIC FINANCE 
SB 1093: property tax; class one; equalization assistance  (Mesnard) Reduces the assessment ratio 
of Class 1 property for property tax purposes to 15.5 percent in 2026 and 15 percent in 2027 and 
after, from 16 percent. Establishes reduced state equalization assistance property tax rates (SE TR) 
for tax years 2022 through 2027 (NOTE: the SETR was eliminat ed in HB 2866). Increas es the 
maximum fire district property tax rate to $3.75 per $100 of assessed valuation for tax year 2024.  
Laws 2022, Chapter 171 
SB 1095: property tax exemptions ; statutory conformity  (Mesnard) Exempts the property of 
veterans with service or nonservice connected disabilities who are Arizona residents from taxation, 
with an allowed exemption amount o f $4,188 multiplied by the percentage of the veteran's 
disability, as rated by the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs, if the person's total assessment does 
not exceed $28,459. The exemption limit for property of widows, widowers, and persons with total 
and permanent disabilities is increased to $4,188, from $3,000, if the person's' total assessment does 
not exceed $28,459, increased from $20,000. Makes various changes to property tax statutes in 
order to conform to proposed changes to property tax exempti ons contained in the state 
Constitution. Conditionally enacted on the state Constitution being amended by a vote of the people 
at the next general election to consolidate and reorganize provisi ons relating to exemptions from 
property taxation. Laws 2022, Chapter 341 
SB 1222: exempt property; tax credit; proceeds (Bowie) Expands the list of property of a debtor that 
is exempt from ex ecution, attachment or sale on any process issued from any court to include the 
refundable portion of any federal or state personal income tax credits from the federal or state 
earned income ta x credits and any additional refundable portion of the federal or state child tax 
credits. The amount of the exemption is the lesser of the total tax refund or the combined amount of 
the federal or state earned income tax credits and the federal or state c hild tax credits claimed on 
the return. Laws 2022, Chapter 346 
SB 1265: property tax liens; foreclosure; notice  (Livingston) States that when a purchaser files an 
action to foreclose a right to redeem, the purchaser shall send notice to the mailing address of the 
property owner (as determined by the county assessor). Establishes that if a purchaser complies with 
all notification requirements, they are deemed to have substantially complied are not required to 
send the notice to any other address. Laws 2022, Chapter 17 
SB 1266: property tax; ad ministration; county assessor (Livingston) Allows the county assessor to 
accept property tax exemption eligibility affidavits electronically and for the taxpayer to file the 
notice of claim electronically .  Previously, these individuals were required to meet personally wi th 
the county assessor or a notary public. Requires a tax officer who accepts an elect ronic notice of 
claim to provide an electronic acknowledgment of receipt. Laws 2022, Chapter 228 
SB 1267: property; classification; primary residence  (Livingston) Specifies, for the purposes of 
property taxation, that a Class 3 property must be used for residential purposes and be occupied by 
the owner.  Requires, for the purposes of  determining limited property va lue, that changes in use 
must be physical and objectively verifiable.  Prohibits a change in the occupant or classification of a 
single-family residence to be considered a change in use for the purposes of changing or correcting 
the tax roll or determining the limited property value. Laws 2022, Chapter 300

<!-- page 320 -->
P a g e  | 38   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
TAXATION AND PUBLIC FINANCE 
SB 1372: TPT; exemptions; motor vehicles; nonresidents (Pace) Exempts sales of motor vehicles to 
nonresidents of the state from the tax on retail classificatio n, and from municipal transaction 
privilege and sales taxes, for a vehicle, trailer or semitrailer that is greater than 10,000 pounds, used 
to transport prope rty for interstate commerce, and is a commercial motor vehicle. Contains a 
conditional enactment. Laws 2022, Chapter 43 
SB 1579: tax corrections act of 2022 (Livingston) Implements corrections to the tax code to correct 
errors, remove obsolete language, and address clarity. Laws 2022, Chapter 235 
SB 1636: use ta x; service vehicles; inventory  (Pace) Specifies for use tax purposes that a dealer 
service vehicle is not considered to be removed from inventory if it is continuously available for sale. 
Laws 2022, Chapter 229

<!-- page 321 -->
P a g e  | 39   County Supervisors Association of Arizona 
 
TRANSPORTATION AND INFRASTRUCTURE 
TRANSPORTATION AND INFRASTRUCTURE 
HB 2481: ADOT; licenses; registration; VLT; aviation (Carroll) Requires the Arizona Department of 
Transportation (ADO T) to establish a system of staggered registration on a month ly basis to 
distribute the work of registering aircraft as uniformly as practic able. Authorizes ADOT to register 
an aircraft for more or less than a 12 -month period and prorate the license tax. A  person is 
authorized to register a fleet of two or more aircraft on an annual basis so that the registrations for 
all aircraft in the fleet  expire in the same month. Exempts h ot air balloons from registering with 
ADOT. Specifies a commercial driver learner's permit is valid for 12 months, instead of six months. 
Increases the maximum weight for an all -terrain vehicle or off -highway vehicle to qu alify for $3 
vehicle license tax to 2,500 pounds, from 1,800 pounds. Laws 2022, Chapter 264 
HB 2673: vehicle operation; licenses; points  (Payne) Authorizes the Arizona Department of 
Transportation (ADOT) to assess points against the person's driving record for only one violation if 
multiple violations result from the same action or course of conduct , for violation of vehicle traffic 
regulations or driving under the influence statutes. Laws 2022, Chapter 383 
HB 2687: urban air mobility committee repeal (Carroll) Extends the time allotted for the Urban Air 
Mobility Study Committee to make their report to the Governor to December 31, 2022. Contains a 
delayed repeal date for the Committee of December 31, 2023. Laws 2022, Chapter 102 
SB 1239: appropriation; widening; I -10 (Shope) Appropriates $400 million f rom the state general 
fund in FY 2023  to the Arizona Department of Transportation (ADOT) to widen Interstate 10 
between Chandler and Casa Grande. ADOT is required to use the monies for construction -related 
activities, including drawing down federal matching monies for the project. Laws 2022, Chapter 218 
SB 1273: two -wheeled motorcycle operation  (Pace) Permits the operator of a two -wheeled 
motorcycle to pa ss another vehicle when stopped and traveling in the same direction.  Allows the 
operator of a two-wheeled motorcycle to move between lanes of traffic if the movement may be 
made safely and if the operator is on streets divided into at least two adjacent , one-way lanes and 
with a speed limit of 45 miles per hour (MPH) or slower and is going 15 MPH or slower. Laws 2022, 
Chapter 42 
SB 1320: intergovernmental public transportation authorit ies; formation (Kerr) Raises the 
population threshold at which a county is prohibited from organizing an  intergovernmental public 
transportation authority from 200,000 persons to 400,000 persons. Laws 2022, Chapter 227 
SB 1333: neighborhood occupantless electric vehicles  (Pace) Authorizes a  neighborhood 
occupantless electric vehicle  to be operated on a highway with a speed limit of 45 miles per hour 
(MPH) or less. Establishes restrictions for a neighborhood occupantless electric vehicle operating on 
a highway with a posted speed limit of more tha n 35 MPH but not exceeding 45 MPH, including 
travelling in the right -hand lane, allowing faster moving vehicles to pass, and displaying a slow -
moving vehicle sign with a reflective surface. Adds neighborhood occupantless electric vehicles to 
various statutes regulating autonomous vehicles. Laws 2022, Chapter 256 
SB 1596 : off-highway vehicle study committee  (Kerr) Establishes a 17 -member Arizona Off -
Highway Vehicle Study Committee (Committee) to collect  and study information regarding off -
highway vehicle issues in Arizona. The Committee is required to submit a report of its activities and 
recommendations to the Governor and th e Legislature by January 1 of each year, and self -repeals 
June 1, 2024.  Laws 2022, Chapter 148
