HB 2859: budget; courts; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 310 
• Arizona Trial and Digital Evidence Fund (AZ Trial Fund): Specifies that jurors serving in superior 
court may be paid through the AZ Trial Fund for lost earnings starting at the beginning of their 
service instead of after more than five days.  Sec. 4 
• Justice of the Peace Compensation : Increases the state’s shar e compensation and employee -
related expenditures in counties with a population of less than 1.5 million people from 19.25  
percent to 40 percent and reduces the county contribution from 80.75  percent to 60 percent.  
Sec. 5 
• Appellate Case Management System: Provides an additional year for the Supreme Court up to 
$2.6 million to implement the appellate case management system. Sec. 6 
HB 2860: budget; criminal justice; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 311 
• Anti-Human Trafficking Grant Fund (AHTF):  Establishes the AHTF to be administered by the 
Arizona Department of Emergency and Mi litary Affairs for programs aimed at reducing human 
trafficking in Arizona.  To be eligible for a grant, the program must either: a) provide analytical 
services to law enforcement agencies; or b) provide services to victims and training to law 
enforcement agencies, prosecutorial agencies, and the public on preventing and identifying 
human trafficking.  Sec. 1 
• Major Incident Division: Establishes the Major Incident Division within Arizona Department of 
Public Safety (DPS) to conduct independent investigation s of critical force incidents.  
Investigators must be certified by the Arizona Peace Officer Standards and Training Board and 
have been  sworn an employee of a federal, state, county, or local law enforcement agency.  
Requires each law enforcement agency in Arizon a to have either the major incident division, a 
regional law enforcement task force or another law enforcement agency perform the criminal 
investigation of any critical force incident in Arizona.   Sec. 3 & 5 
• Criminal Justice Enhancement Fund (CJEF): Modifies the allocation of CJEF monies, eliminates 
the distribution to the peace officers’ training fund (18.97 percent), and increases the percentage 
of funds to the other recipients i ncluding funding to county sheriffs for enhanced jail facilities, 
the Supreme Court to r educe juvenile crime, county attorneys to enhanc e prosecution efforts, 
and drug treatment services to adult probationers.  Sec. 6 
• State Aid for Juvenile Dependency Fund  (SAJDF): Establishes the SAJDF for County Public 
Defenders, Legal Defenders and Cont ract Indigent Defense Council for processing juveni le 
dependency cases. The SAJDF shall be administered by the Arizona Criminal Justice Commission 
(ACJC) and distributed to counties by September 1 of each fiscal year based on the three -year 
average of the total juvenile dependency case filings in the count y that exceeds the average for 
FY 2013 through FY 2015. Sec. 7 & 8

STATE BUDGET ADJUSTMENTS 
HB 2861: budget; environment; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 312 
• Water Infrastructure and Commerce Grant Fund (WICGF): Establishes the WICGF consisting 
of legislative appropriations, federal monies, and private  donations, in law and will be 
administered by the Arizona Commerce Authority.  Allows grant monies to be used to design and 
construct water infrastructure for entities that are either:  
o An employer with more than 250 employees located in a county with between 400,000 and 
one million residents; or  
o A public service corporation that provi des water servic e and that is acting on behalf of an 
employer described above. Sec. 1 
HB 2862: budget; general appropriations act; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 313 
• Election Security Funding : Appropriates $1.5 million in FY 2023 to the Department of 
Administration (ADOA). $1 million of the allocation is for one or more county r ecorders to test 
ballot paper in tabulation systems with specific security features. Requires the ADOA to compile 
a report from the selected counties on the recommended security features by May 1, 2023. The 
remaining $500,000 is to procure, install, and mainta in 24/7 photo and video surveillance  for 
secure ballot boxes in: Cochise (6 boxes), Yuma (7 boxes), Pinal (3 boxes) counties. The ADOA is 
required to assist counties with the procurement of the drop boxes. The drop boxes are required 
to: only accept one ballot at a time, log the receipt and generate a paper receipt, and reject ballots 
if the surveillance equipment is nonoperational. Sec. 5 
• Fire Incident Management System Grants: $6.1 million to enable the sta tewide deployment of 
a secure incident management platform to fire and law enforcement agencies. Sec. 5 
• County Total Healthcare Payments : Sets the county payment for the Budget Neutrality 
Compliance Fund (BNCF) at $4.3 million for FY 2023. Sets the county payment for the Arizona 
Long-term Care Program at $352.7 milli on. This includes an 11  percent provider rate increase 
from Arizona Long Term Care System (ALTCS) Elderly and Physically Disability (EPD) providers, 
which cost counties $19.4 million ongoing. Sec. 10 
• Attorney General (AG) Elections Case Funding: Provides that $500,000 from the AG’s FY 2022 
budget is non lapsing for the purpose of legal costs associated with the pursuit of criminal 
prosecutions and for defense in civil cases regarding elections. Sec. 13 
• County Sheriff Interoperability for School Sa fety: $20.0 million in FY 20 23 to county sheriffs 
for school safety interoperability. Sec. 5

STATE BUDGET ADJUSTMENTS 
• Out of County Tuition: Appropriates $1.08 million from the state general fund (GF) for rural 
county reimbursement subsidies for counties without an established community college district. 
Apache County receives $699,300 and Greenlee County recei ves $ 383,600. Sec. 20  This is a 
decrease of $500,000 over last year. Note: Additional changes to the out of county tuition formula 
are made in HB 2864. Sec. 20 
• Cochise Community College First Responders Academy: Appropriates $6.25 million to Cochise 
Community College to construct an indoor shooting range and burn tower for the first responder 
academy. Sec. 20  
• Rural County Attorney Diversion Funding: Appropriates $10.0 million to the Arizona Criminal 
Justice Commission (ACJC) for county attorney diversion programs in counties with fewer than 
900,000 persons for the purposes of establishing evidence -based diversion practices. Requires 
ACJC to submit a report to Joint Legislative Budget Committee on the outcomes of the programs 
by September 30, 2023. Funds are non-lapsing. Sec. 24 
• State Aid for Ju venile Dependency Proceedings Fund : Allocates $2.0 million ongoing to the 
ACJC  for grants to defray the cost of juvenile dependency proceedings to counties. Sec. 24 
• County Attorneys Fund: Continues to appropriate $973,700 from the State Aid to the County 
Attorneys Fund to support county attorney’s offices in improving criminal case processing.        
Sec. 24 
• Indigent Defense Fund : Continues to appropriate $700,000 from the State Aid to I ndigent 
Defense Fund to the ACJC for state aid to indigent defense.       Sec. 24 
• County Participation; Child Support Enforcement:  Appropriates $8.54 million for county 
participation in child support enforcement to the Department of Economic Security (DES).  This 
amount is unchanged from last year. Sec. 29 
• WQARF Annual Report: The Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ) is required 
to report on the budget and activities of WQARF to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee 
(JLBC) by September 1, 2022. Additionally requires the WQARF Ad visory Board to submit a 
report on the progress of projects to JLBC by October 1, 2022. Sec. 33 
• State Forester; Environmental County Grants: Continues to appropriate $250,000 to the State 
Forester for county environmental projects. Sec. 38 
• Hazardous Vegetation Removal: Appropriates $3.0 million to the state Department of Forestry 
and Fire Management for hazardous vegetation rem oval. This is unchanged from last year.          
Sec. 38 
• Forest Health: Provides $2.9 million in additional ongoing funding to the Department of Forestry 
and Fire Management, including an additional 17 FTE for 19 thousand acres of U .S. Forest 
Service Land Thinning and 5.5 FTE for additional Fire Marshall staff. Additionally, provides $65.0 
million in one-time, non-lapsing, appropriations for wildfire expenses, including $3.0 million for 
the Woodbury Fire. Sec. 38 
• Gila River Nonnative Species Eradication: Appropriates $5.0 million in FY 2023 through            FY 
2025 (ARPA dollars in FY 2024 and FY 2025), for nonnative vegetation specie s eradication 
projects along with Gila River between the City of Avondale and the bridge over the Gila River 
at SR-85. Sec. 38

STATE BUDGET ADJUSTMENTS 
• County Fair Livestock and Agricultural Promotion : Appropriates $6.0 million to the County 
Fairs Livest ock and Agriculture Promotion Fund. This amount is $2.0 million more than the 
previous year. Sec. 41 
• County Tuberculosis Provider Care an d Control: Maintains a $590,700 appropriation to the 
Department of Health Services to support county tuberculosis programs. Sec. 44 
• Statewide Cyber Security Grants : Appropriates $10.0 million to the state’s existing cybe r 
security grant program for school districts and local governments to enhance their cyber 
security. Sec. 47 
• Housing Grants: Deposits $60.0 million in the Housing Trust Fund for grants to projects in cities, 
towns, and counties. Includes  a provision that $20. 0 million must be for projects in co unties 
outside of Maricopa and Pima counties, and an additional $4.0 million much be distributed to the 
Navajo and Hopi tribal nations. Sec. 49  
• Homeless Services Grant Program: Appropriates $10.0 million to the Department of Housing to 
establish a grant p ilot for counties, cities , and towns’ homeless services programs designed to 
reduce homelessness. Sec. 49  
• Automobile Theft Grants: Appropriates $957,700 for local grants and requires the Automobile 
Theft Authority to iss ue grants to areas with automobile t heft problems to combat automobile 
theft and to pay for 75  percent of the employee -related expenses for county and municipal 
officers that participate in the Arizona Vehicle Theft Task Force. Sec. 51 
• County Judicial Reimburseme nts: Continues to provide $187,900 t o the Arizona Supreme 
Court to reimburse counties for state grand juries and capital post -conviction relief (PCR).  The 
state grand jury reimbursement is limited to $97,900 and the PCR reimbursement is limited to 
$90,000. Sec. 52 
• Judicial Salary Increases : The salary for a superior court judge increases from $149,400 to 
$164,700 in FY 2023 and $180,000 in FY 2024. Per statute, increases salaries for superior court 
commissioners and justices of the peace. Sec. 52 
• State Share of Supe rior Court Judges : Provides $24.9 mi llion for 50  percent of the superior 
court judge salaries and employee related expenditure costs for all counties. The amount reflects 
the state’s share of the increased judicial salaries. Sec. 52 
• County Probation Maintenance of Effort Requirement: Requires that in order to receive state 
aid for probation, counties maintain FY 2020 expenditure levels. This language is unchanged 
from last year. Sec. 52 
• Probation Officer Salary Increases:  Allocates $1.23 million for probation officer salary 
increases of 2.5  percent in FY 2 023. Requires that if counties approve step or inflation salary 
increases in FY 2023  which increase the state’s share above the amount appropriated , the 
Legislature intends that the counties absorb any additional cost to the state in FY 2023 and 
beyond.          Sec. 52 
• County-by-County Probation Officer Report: Continues to require the Administrative Office of 
the Courts (AOC) to report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee (JLBC) and the Governor’s 
Office of Strategic Pl anning and Budgeting (OSPB), by November 1, 2022, the number of 
authorized and filled probation positions on a coun ty-by-county basis, along with total receipts 
and expenditures by county.     Sec. 52

STATE BUDGET ADJUSTMENTS 
• Probation Salary Increases Re port: Requires by November 1, 2022, the Administrative Office 
of the Courts (A OC) report to the Joint Legislative B udget Committee ( JLBC) on the county -
approved salary adjustments provided to probation officers since November 1, 2021. Sec. 52 
• ADJC Cost Shift: Appropriates $8.45 million from the Department of Juvenile Corrections Local 
Cost Sharing Fund established by A .R.S. § 41 -2833. A.R.S. § 41 -2832 requires that the 
department assess the fee on only Maricopa and Pima counties, but does not outline the amount 
to be charged to each county. Sec. 53 
• Border Strike Task Force – Local Support:  Appropriates $12.2 million to the D epartment of 
Public Safety (DPS) for the border strike task force initiative. Requires that $11.7 million be used 
to fund local law enforcement officers’ positions within the bor der strike task force. Stipulates 
those cities, towns, counties, or other  entities that enter into an agreement with DPS to 
participate in the task force provide at least 25  percent of the funding. Requires the remaining 
$500,000 be used to fund grants for locali ties to cover costs associated with prosecuting and 
imprisoning individuals charged with border related crimes. Sec. 77 
• DPS Cadet and Vacant FTE: Allocates $0.5 million to the Department of Public Safety (DPS) to 
pay cadet housing costs in training. Provides $10.1 million ongoing, $450,000 one-time, to fill 69 
vacant FTE positions (57 sworn, 12 civilian) at DPS. Add itionally, requires that $16.3 million of 
the department’s Gang and Immigration Intelligen ce Team Enforcement Mission ( GIITEM) 
funding be used for 100 DPS personnel to assist with strict border enforcement and investigative 
actions. Sec. 77 
• Justice of the Peace (JP) Salaries:  Appropriates $2.8 million to the Arizona State Treasurer to 
cover the state’s share of JP salaries.  This amount is $1.6 million above last year to cover the cost 
of the salary increase and the restoration of the state/county cost share at 40/60. Sec. 88 
• Law Enforcement Boating Safety Fund:   Appropriates $2.18 million to be allocated to county 
law enforcement agencies in counties which had a law enforcement and boating safety program 
in existence prior to July 1, 1990 (Apache, Coconino, Gila, La Paz, Maricopa, Mohave, Navajo, 
Yuma counties). This amount is unchanged from last year. Sec. 88 
• Eastern Arizona Water Project A ssistance Grants: Appropriates $10.0 million to  the Water 
Infrastructure Finance Authority (WIFA), of which $6.0 million is allocated to assistance for cities 
and towns that provide water in Navajo and Apache counties. The remaining $4 .0 million is for 
financial assistance to irrigation districts in Cochise and Graham counties ($2.0 million each 
county). WIFA is required to develop separate grant programs and distribute the fund prior to 
June 30, 2023. Sec. 96 
• Rural Water Studies: Appropriates $1.2 million to assess local water use needs and to develop 
plans for sustainable futu re water supplies in rural areas outside of the state’s active 
management areas. This amount is unchanged from last year. Sec. 97 
• State Building Debt Payoff:  Appropriates $93.5 million in FY 2022 for retire d financing 
agreements on state buildings. Sec. 99 
• Pension Debt Repayment : Allocates $1.14 billion for  the state’s Public Safety Personnel 
Retirement System ( PSPRS) and Corrections Officer Re tirement Plan ( CORP) pension debt 
repayment in FY 2022. Total estimated savings of $99.9 million annually to the state. Figure does 
not include the CORP account for probation officers o r Elected Officials ’ Retirement Plan 
(EORP). Budget spreadsheet indicates a $60 million deposit into EORP in FY 2023. Sec. 105

• Pension Assumptions Report: Requires the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) 
to submit a report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee (JLBC) and the Office of Strategic 
Planning and Budgeting ( OSPB) on the system’s curre nt actuarial assum ptions and potential 
future changes. Sec. 105 
• Direct Appropriations to Counties (In-Lieu Lottery Revenue): Appropriates $7.15 million from 
the state general fund  to the Arizona Department of Administration (ADOA) to be equ ally 
distributed to all 13 counties under 900,000 persons (Apache, Cochise, Coconino, Gila, Graham, 
Greenlee, La Paz, Mohave, Navajo, Pinal, Santa Cruz, Yavapai, and Yuma). Each qualifying county 
receives $550,050. Sec. 106 
• Graham County Assistance: Appropriates $500,000 from the state general fund to the Arizona 
Department of Ad ministration (ADOA) for distribution t o Graham County for maintenance of 
essential county services. Sec. 106 
• Elected Officials Retirement Plan (EORP) Relief: As session law, appropriates $3.0 million from 
the state general fund to be distributed equally across counties with a population under 300,000. 
Each qualifying county receives $250,000. Sec. 106 
• County Reentry Planning Services Program:  As session law, appropriate s $7.0 million to the 
Arizona Department of Ad ministration ( ADOA) for distributio n to counties to establish a 
coordinated reentry planning services program. Does not indicate the allocation of funds to 
particular counties. Sec. 106 
• Cochise County Jail:  Appropriates $20.0 million from the Border Sec urity Fund to Cochise 
County to construct a new county jail facility. S tipulates that the funds are intended to be a 20  
percent match, up to $20 million. Funds may not be distributed to the county until Cochise 
County has submitted a report that the county has ra ised the remaining 80 percent required to 
fund the jail to the Office of Strategic Planning and Budgeting ( OSPB), Joint Legislative Budget 
Committee ( JLBC) and Arizona Department of Administrat ion ( ADOA). Unexpended funds 
revert to the Border Security Fund on June 30, 2027. Sec. 110 
• Local Border Enforcement Reimbursement:  Allocates $15.0 million from the Border Security 
Fund to reimbur se county or local enforcement for costs incurred by the county or loca l law 
enforcement agency for participating in a program with the United States immig ration and 
customs enforcement pursuant to 8 United States code section 1357(g). Sec. 110 
• Antihuman Trafficking Grants: Designates $10.0 million for deposit in the antihuman trafficking 
grant fund. Of that amount, $8.0 million may be for grants for local law enforcement agencies up 
to $500,000 per agency for programs that reduce human trafficking. Sec. 110 
• Border-related Prosecution Grants: Allocates $30.0 million from the B order Security Fund to 
distribute to cities, towns , or counties for costs associated  with prosecuting and imprisoning 
individuals charged with drug trafficking, human smuggling, illegal immigration, and other 
border-related crimes. Sec. 110

fund for one-time deputy and detention officer payments to the Department of Administration.  
o $48.3 million for $10,000 payments to line-level deputy and detention officers employed as 
of May 1, 2022, for quarterly installments over two years. Sheriffs must report by    August 
30, 2023, and August 30, 2024, on the actual use and most recent staffing and retention data 
for positions receiving the payment. Requires the Arizona Department of Administration 
(ADOA) to submit summ ary rep ort to the Joint Legislative Budget Comm ittee (JLBC) and

year. 
• $5.1 million for $5,000 payments to line-level deputy and detention officers hired between May 
1, 2022, an d May 1,  2023.  T he county shall distribute the payment to eligible employees in  
quarterly installments over one year.  On June 1, 2023, the Arizona Department of 
Administration (ADOA) shall distribute the funding to the counties based on the actual number  
of hires, as demonstrated by each county.  If the amount is insufficient for a $5,000 per employee 
payment, ADOA shall distribute a prorated amount.  On or before June 30, 2023, ADOA is

110  
• DPS Southern Border Coordinated Resp onse Center: Allocates $15.0 million from the Border 
Security Fund to the Department of Public Safety (DPS) for the construction costs of a southern 
border coordinated response center. Sec. 110 
• State Employee Salary Adjustments: Provides funding to state agencies for salary adjustments 
of at least 10  percent, depending on the agency. The Departme nt of Corrections ( DOC) and 
Department of Juvenile Corrections (DJC) staff will receive an average of 20 percent increases, 
while Department of Public Safety (DPS) staff will receive and average of 15 percent increases.  
Sec. 124 
• Voter Registration Roll Accuracy  Funding: Appropriates $6.0 million in FY 2024 to the 
Treasurer’s Office for distribution to county recorders in counties with a population of 200,000 
or more persons. Distributed to each county based on population. Requires funds to be used by 
county recorders to review the accuracy of the voter registration rolls, including whether  any 
registrations should be canceled. Sec. 129 
• Ballot Paper Funding: Appropriates $5.0 million in FY 2024 and $6.0 million in FY 2025 to the 
Treasurer’s Office to distribute to  counties on a proportional basis to county recorders that 
purchase ballot paper that uses specific security features, including watermarks or unique ballot 
identifiers, or both. Sec. 130

• Medicare Local Cost Sharing Compliance: As session law, states that  the Arizona Health Care 
Cost Containment System ( AHCCCS) must “transfer to the counties the portion, if any, as may 
be necessary to comply with sec tion 10201(c)(6) of the patient protection and affordable care 
act” for FY 2022 – 2023 on or before December 31, 2023.  This language is standard to comply 
with federal cost sharing requirements. Sec. 16 
• Arizona Health Care Cos t Containment System (AHCCCS) Disproportionate Share Hospital 
(DSH) Payments:  
o Establishes the FY 2023 DSH payments as follows: 
▪ $113.8 million for a qualifying non-state-operated public hospital, of which   $4.2 
million is distributed to the Maricopa County Special Health Care District (MIHS), 
and the remaining federal portion is distributed to the state general fund;  
▪ $28.47 million for the Arizona State Hospital (ASH), of which the federal portion 
is distributed into the state general fund; and 
▪ $884,800 for private qualifying DSH hospitals, which are hospitals that meet the 
mandatory definition of disproportionate share to qualifying hospitals, as defined 
by the Social Security Act, or those hospitals that are located in Yuma County and 
contain at least 300 beds. Sec. 7 
o Requires MIHS and ASH to each provide a Certified Public Expense form for the amount 
of qualifying DSH expenditures to AHCCCS by May 1, 2023, for MIHS, and March 31, 
2023, for ASH. Sec 7 
o Requires that further allocations of disproportionate share hospital payments are made 
available to private hospitals in c ounties with fewer than 400,000 persons first, then to 
counties with between 400,000 and 900,000 persons, and then to counties with a 
population of 900,000 or more. Sec. 15

• Out of County Tuition Formula Change : Beginning in FY 2023, changes how reimbursement 
payments from counties who are not part of an organized community college district to local 
community college districts are calculated for students who atte nd classes in a community 
college district (CCD) by: 
1. Dividing the CCD's prior year pri mary property tax levy by the CCD's current year full -
time equivalent student (FTSE) number; and

county for the preceding year. Sec. 1

• County Jail Education Program: Extends age requirements for county jail educatio n programs 
to all prisoners who are 21 years old or younger and who do not have a high school diploma or a 
general equivalency diploma.  Previously specified prisoners who are 18 years of age or younger 
or individuals with disabilities who are 21 years old or younger. Sec. 7 
• Community College Adult Education Workforce Development Program (P rogram): 
Establishes the Program  within the State Board of Education to provide adult learners with 
integrated education and training programs to earn a high school diplo ma or high school 
equivalency diploma, and an industry -recognized credential or commun ity college degree.  
Allows community colleges tha t can provide education and training leading to a diploma, 
credential, or degree to apply. Sec. 2 
• County Aid for Equaliz ation Assistance for Edu cation: Repeals language in § 15 -971 outlining 
how the “County Aid for Equalization Assistance for Education” is computed and removing 
consideration of counties and associated property tax rates from calculations of “State Aid for 
Equalization Assistance for Education.” Part of the repeal of the state equalization ta x rate. Sec. 
13 
• State Equalization Assistance Property Tax Rate: Repeals language in § 41-1276 outlining the 
“State Equalization Assistance Property Tax Rate” in future tax years. These funds to education 
are backfilled with state general fund resources to K-12 education. Sec. 19 
• Limits on County Primary Property Tax Levy: Makes technical changes to statutory references 
of local levy limits to comply with state equalization tax rate elimination. Sec. 21

any county fiscal obligation from any source of county revenue designated by the county. 
Specifies that a county may not use more than $1.25 million for purposes other than the 
purposes of th e revenue source. Requires counties that use this tool to report its usage to the 
Joint Legislative Budget Committee (JLBC) by October 1, 2022.  Sec. 1

HB 2871: budget; tax; revisions; distributions; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 321 
• ADOR Tax System Modernization Project Advisory Committee : Establishes the Tax System 
Modernization Project Advisory Commit tee to serve as a liaison between the depart ment and 
users of the tax system. Members of the committee include one representative of a county, 
appointed by the Arizona Department of Revenue (ADOR) director. Sec. 2 
• ADOR Tax System Modernization Project Fund and Local Cost Sharing Fee: As permanent law, 
establishes the Arizona Department of Revenue ( ADOR) Integrated Tax System Project Fund 
and requires the director to assess a fee on counties, cities, towns, and Council of Governments 
(COGs) that receive revenue pursuant to 42 -6105 and Rural Tran sit Authorities ( RTAs) in 
counties with more than 800,000 persons. Requires funds from the additional 0.6 percent from 
education and the smart and safe fund (marijuana excise tax) to be deposited into the fund. 
Requires that the funds be used solely for costs related to developing and implementing the 
integrated tax system modernization project.  The fee is assessed from FY 2023 through             FY 
2028. Allows the fee to be paid from any funding source of a county, city, or town. Sec. 6 
• ADOR Tax System Modernization Local Cost Sharing Fee : Sets the Arizona Department of 
Revenue (ADOR) Tax System fee at no mor e than $5,388,200 for counties, cities, t owns, Rural 
Transit Authorities (RTAs), and Council of Governments (COGs). This fee structure is the same 
used for the prior ADOR cost sharing fee. Each county’s individual share of the aggregate county 
share is set using their 2020 census population factor. The aggregate county share of the fee is 
determined through the following procedure over the prior two fiscal years: 
1) Calculate the aggregate amount distributed to counties from:  
a) A.R.S. § 42-5029 (transaction privilege tax [TPT] distribution base)  
b) A.R.S. § 42-6103 (county general fund excise tax) 
c) A.R.S. § 42-6107 (county transportation excise tax for roads) 
d) A.R.S. § 42-6108 & 42-6108.01 (tax on hotels – Pima only) 
e) A.R.S. § 42-6109 & 42-6109.01 (jail facility excise tax – Maricopa only) 
f) A.R.S. § 42-6110 (use tax on electricity) 
g) A.R.S. § 42-6111 (county capital projects) 
h) A.R.S. § 42-6112 (county excise tax for county judgment bonds) 
i) A.R.S. § 42-6112 (county excise tax on coal mining) 
2) Calculate the aggregate amount distributed to counties, cities and towns, Maricopa Association 
of Governments (MAG), and Pima Association of Governments (PAG) from: 
a) All taxes listed under step one 
b) A.R.S. § 42-6001 (city excise taxes) 
c) A.R.S. § 43-206 (urban revenue sharing) 
d) A.R.S. § 42-6105 (MAG transportation tax) 
e) A.R.S. § 42-6106 (PAG transportation tax)

STATE BUDGET ADJUSTMENTS 
3) Calculate what perc entage the aggregate amount calculated under step 1 is of the aggregate 
amount calculated under step 2 and apply this percentage to the total fee. Total county fee is 
distributed across counties based on the county’s s hare of the state’s population accordi ng to 
the 2020 census. Sec. 18 
• Public Infrastructure Improvements for Manufacturing: Increases the cap of state resources 
that can be paid to a city, town or county for the purpose of funding up to 80 percent of the cost 
of publ ic infrastructure improvements for the benefit of a manufacturing facility from $50.0 
million to $100.0 million. Sec. 5 
• State General Fund TPT Transfers : Transfers the following amounts from the state’s share of 
transaction privilege tax (TPT) from the general fund: $946.1 million to the State Highway Fund 
and Aviatio n Funds for various transportation projects (described in capital outlay budget 
reconciliation bill ( BRB)), $209.2 million to the Border Security Fund, $425.0 million to the 
state’s Budget Stabilization Fund, and $38.2 million to the State Parks Reven ue Fund. Contains 
legislative intent language to clarify that these allocations do not impact the county, city and 
town shared TPT distributions. Sec. 20 
• Aircraft License Tax Assessment: Restricts, for the purpose of valuing aircrafts for the Aircraft 
License Tax, the growth in the value of the aircraft to growth in the Consumer Price Index (CPI). 
Requires that for FY 2022, the value of the aircraft is benchmarked to the value in 2019. Sec. 1 
• Agricultural OHV Sales Tax Exemptions:  Amends statute to exempt off -highway vehicles, as  
defined in § 28-1171, to function as tractor or to tow tractor-drawn implements, from the state 
transaction privilege tax base. Sec. 7-9, 30 
• License Tax Rate: Provides individuals that saw a larger increase in their license tax in FY 2022 
prior to the effective date of the bill shall be provided a credit towards their FY 2023 license tax 
payment. Sec. 1, 23 
• Containment Structure Sales Tax Exemptions: Exempts the income derived from a contract to 
install containment structures (defined) from transaction privilege tax base. Sec. 7-11, 30 
 
HB 2872: budget; transportation; 2022-2023 (Cobb) - Laws 2022, Chapter 322 
• State Match Advantage for Rural Transportation Fund (SMART Fund): Establishes the SMART 
Fund and specifies that 20 percent of the fund shall be allocated to counties with a population of 
100,000 persons or more and another 20 percent will be allocated to counties with a population 
of less than 100,000 persons.  Prohibits counties with a population of more than one million 
people from receiving SMART Fund monies.  Provides requirements for political subdivisions to 
apply for an award and how the board may give preference to applicants that demonstrate 
matching monies or ability to partner with other entities to deliver the project.  SMART Funds 
may be used for: 
1. Reimbursing up to 50  percent of the costs associated with developing and submitting a 
federal grant application; 
2. Providing a match for a federal grant; and  
3.  Reimbursing design and other engineering services expenditures that meet federal 
standards for projects eligible for a federal grant.  Sec. 3

COUNTY RELATED VETOED LEGISLATION 
COUNTY RELATED VETOED LEGISLATION 
HB 2617: voter registration; cancellations; causes (Chaplik) Specifies w hen a county recorder 
receives information that a registered voter is not a U.S. citizen, has been issu ed a driver license or 
nonoperating identification license from another state, or is otherwise not qualified to vote, the 
county recorder is required to cancel the person's voter registration. Requires the county recorder 
to notify the person whose registr ation will be canceled in 90 days unless the person provides 
satisfactory evidence that the person is qualified prior to cancelling a voter registration.  Specifies if 
satisfactory evidence is not provided within 90 days, the county recorder is required to  cancel the 
registration and notify the county attorney and Attorney General for possible investigation. Each 
month the Secretary of State is required to  compare the statewide voter registration database to 
the driver license database, and to notify the ap propriate county recorder if a person who is 
registered to vote in that county has changed the person's residence address or is not a U.S. citizen. 
Each month the county recorder is required to compare the county's voter registration database to 
the Social  Security Administration database and other government databases to ensure that 
registered voters are qualified to vote.  AS VETOED BY GOVERNOR. In his ve to message, the 
Governor stated that the language was too vague for the county recorder to implement and leaves 
the election system open to bad actors. Governor’s Letter. 
HB 2685: transportation tax; election; Maricopa County (Carroll) Beginning January 1, 2026, if 
approved by the voters at a countywide election, a county with a population of  three 3 million or 
more persons (Maricopa) is required to levy a transpor tation excise tax of up to 10 percent of the 
transaction privilege tax rate as of January 1, 1990. The tax levied will be in effect for 25 years. The 
regional planning agency in the county is required to develop and adopt a multimodal transportation 
plan. The plan must specify the distribution of net revenues from the ta x levy, with 59.6 percent of 
revenues distributed to the Regional Area Road Fund and 40.4 percent of revenues to the Public 
Transportation Fund. No more than 14 percent of the revenues may b e spent on light rail systems. 
The plan is required to allocate at least $90 million for implementation of commercial motor vehicle 
parking by December 31, 2049. During the period beginning four years before an existing county 
transportation excise tax would otherwise be discontinued and ending three years before that date, 
the Maricopa County Board of Supervisors is required to call a countywide election for the extension 
and levy of a county transportation excise tax as authorized by this legislation. Est ablishes 
requirements for the ballot and publicity pamphlet for the election. AS VETOED BY GOVERNOR. In 
his veto message, the Governor stated that now is not the time to ask voters in Arizona to tax 
themselves, and that this legislation is not responsible and transparent. Governor’s Letter 
SB 1592: governor's declaration; fiscal impact analysis (Gray) In any year in which the Governor has 
initially declared a state of war emergency or state of emergency for a public health emergency, the 
State Treasurer is prohibited from withholding any amount from the Arizona Convention Center 
Development Fund from an eligible city for tha t year. In conducting the analysis or estimate of the 
economic impact of any project eligible for monies from the Fund in subsequent years following the 
emergency declaration, the Auditor General is required to assume that the eligible city satisfied the 
minimum required attendance in the year of the emergency declaration and the year following that 
declaration, and that the incremental revenues to the general fund in any year of the initial 
emergency declaration and the year following that declaration at l east equaled the amount of 
distributions by the state. Retroactive to January 1, 2020. AS VETOED BY GOVERNOR. In his veto 
message, the Governor stated that while the goal is to create fiscal certainty, the legislation attempts 
to solve a problem that does not currently exist. Governor’s Letter

LEGISLATIVE BALLOT MEASURES 
LEGISLATIVE BALLOT MEASURES 
During the 2022 Legislative Session the Legislature passed several ballot propositions to be 
considered by the voters during the 2022 General Election.  Listed below are the measures that were 
sent to the Secretary of State to be put on the ballot , and the Legislative Council Analysis adopted 
on Wednesday, July 20, 2022.  To view the current 2022 Ballot Measure Arguments please click 
here. 
HCR 2015 initiatives; supermajority vote; requirement (Dunn)  
Ballot Measure Name: Proposition 132  
Submits to the voters a  constitutional change that requires any statewide initiative or referendum 
for a ta x to be approved  by at least 60 percent of the voters.  Measure Language ,                                        
Legislative Council Analysis 
SCR 1011 constitutional property tax exemptions (Mesnard) 
Ballot Measure Name: Proposition 130 
Submits to the voters a constitutional change to allow the Legislature to determine the property tax 
exemptions and specifies that an Arizona resident is eligible to only use one category for property 
tax exemption s (widow, widower, person with a disability or  veteran with a disability).  Measure 
Language, Legislative Council Analysis 
SCR 1012 voting; voter identification (Mesnard) 
Ballot Measure Name: Proposition 309 
Submits to the voters a statutory requirement that all early ballots would be required to include an 
Arizona driver’s license number or non -operating ID ; the last 4 of SSN or unique ID ; voter 
identification number ; date of birth ; and signature of the voter to be counted  by 2024.                   
Measure Language, Legislative Council Analysis 
SCR 1024 lieutenant governor; joint ticket (Mesnard) 
Ballot Measure Name: Proposition 131 
Submits to the voters a co nstitutional change to create the Office of Lieutenant Governor starting 
in 2027 and makes the Lieutenant Governor the first in line of succession , instead of the Secretary 
of State.  Requires each nominee for Governor to name a Lieutenant Governor nominee  at least 60 
days before the general election to run  on a joint ticket with the Governor at the general election. 
Measure Language, Legislative Council Analysis 
SCR 1049 fire districts; funding; TPT Increment (Boyer) 
Ballot Measure Name: Proposition 310 
Submits to the voters an additional statutory transaction privilege tax of 0.1 percent for a period of 
20 years to be deposited in the Fire District safety Fund (Fund).  Outlines how monies in the Fund 
would be disbursed to fire districts in Arizona. Measure Language, Legislative Council Analysis
